The approximation property for locally compact quantum groups Thanks: This work was supported by EPSRC grants EP/T03064X/1 and EP/T030992/1. For the purpose of open access, the authors have applied a CC BY public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript version arising. No data were created or analysed in this study.
Matthew Daws, Jacek Krajczok, Christian Voigt

Abstract
. We study the Haagerup–Kraus approximation property for locally compact quantum groups, generalising and unifying previous work by Kraus–Ruan and Crann. Along the way we discuss how multipliers of quantum groups interact with the C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -algebraic theory of locally compact quantum groups. Several inheritance properties of the approximation property are established in this setting, including passage to quantum subgroups, free products of discrete quantum groups, and duals of double crossed products. We also discuss a relation to the weak ∗ operator approximation property. For discrete quantum groups, we introduce a central variant of the approximation property, and relate this to a version of the approximation property for rigid C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -tensor categories, building on work of Arano–De Laat–Wahl.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L67, Secondary 22D55, 43A30

1.
Introduction

The approximation property (AP) for locally compact groups, introduced by Haagerup and Kraus
[
32
]
, can be viewed as an analogue of Grothendieck’s approximation property for Banach spaces
[
28
]
. It belongs to a family of widely studied analytical properties
like amenability, weak amenability, and the Haagerup property. In fact, AP is a weakening of weak amenability and thus a very loose form of amenability.
It is known that AP passes from locally compact groups to their lattices and vice versa, and that it has better permanence properties with respect to standard constructions
like extensions and free products, in comparison to weak amenability.

It was an open problem for a long time to exhibit examples of exact groups without AP. In the remarkable paper
[
48
]
, Lafforgue and de la Salle
proved that
\operatorname{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
fails to have AP, thus confirming a conjecture in
[
32
]
. Building on this result, it was shown later
by Haagerup, Knudby and De Laat that a connected Lie group has AP if and only if all simple factors in its Levi decomposition have real rank at most one

[
29
]
,
[
30
]
,
[
31
]
.

The approximation property has a wide range of applications. As shown by Haagerup-Kraus
[
32
]
, in the case of discrete groups there is a connection between AP and the slice map property (or equivalently, the operator approximation property) of the associated crossed products. It was recently proven in the full generality of locally compact groups that AP implies exactness
[
64
]
(see also
[
15
]
), which makes it relevant to a number of problems in operator algebras. Let us also mention that AP was shown to be equivalent to a non-commutative version of Fejér’s theorem
[
15
]
, and used to prove results concerning convolution operators on
\operatorname{L}^{p}(G)

[
12
,
21
]
.

Amenability, weak amenability and the Haagerup property have also been studied extensively in the broader setting of locally compact quantum groups, see
[
9
]

for a survey. An interesting new feature in the quantum setting is the interplay between discrete quantum groups, their Drinfeld doubles, and
the associated
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories
[
22
]
. In fact, the central versions of amenability, the Haagerup property, weak amenability and central property (T) for discrete quantum groups have been recast at the level of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories
[
57
]
, thus building a natural bridge to the study
of subfactors.

In the present paper we undertake a systematic study of the approximation property for locally compact quantum groups. Kraus and Ruan introduced a version of the approximation property for Kac algebras in
[
42
]
, requiring
the existence of a net in the Fourier algebra
\A(\mathbb{G})
such that the associated net of completely bounded operators on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
converges to the identity in the stable point weak
∗
-topology of
\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. Crann studied this property for general locally compact quantum
groups, and showed for example that in the presence of this property, amenability is equivalent to coamenability of the dual quantum group
[
14
, Corollary 7.4]
.

Our starting point is the original work by Haagerup and Kraus. We say that a locally compact quantum group has AP if it admits a net of elements in the Fourier algebra
\A(\mathbb{G})
which converges weak
∗
to
\mathbbm{1}
in the space of left CB multipliers
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. We show that this definition is in fact equivalent to the definition of AP used in
[
42
]
,
[
14
]
, thereby verifying a conjecture in
[
42
]
. This result was proved by Haagerup and Kraus in the case of locally compact groups (
[
32
, Theorem 1.9]
). Our methods are necessarily different, as we cannot work with points or compactly supported functions. Along the way, we obtain a useful alternative description of the weak
∗
-topology on the space of left CB multipliers (Theorem
3.8
). We prove that working with left or right multipliers does not change the theory, and that passing from a quantum group to its opposite or commutant preserves AP. We also show that if a quantum group
\mathbb{G}
has the AP exhibited by a net which is uniformly bounded in the norm of
\A(\mathbb{G})
(resp.
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
), then the dual is coamenable (resp. the quantum group is weakly amenable).

We then derive a number of permanence properties of AP in analogy to the classical setting. In particular, we show that AP passes to closed quantum subgroups
of locally compact quantum groups, and to duals of double crossed products. This includes the passage to direct products of quantum groups
as a special case. In the setting of discrete quantum groups we verify that AP is inherited by free products and direct limits of directed systems
of discrete quantum groups with injective connecting maps. We also introduce a central version of the approximation property for discrete quantum groups and show that it is related to a natural notion of AP for rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories, building on work
of Arano–De Laat–Wahl
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
.

Using our results we can provide a range of examples of quantum groups with and without AP. For instance, the Drinfeld double of
\operatorname{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
does not have AP, and the free
product
\operatorname{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})\star\widehat{\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)}
does not have AP. Examples with AP can be obtained via free products as well, for example
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\star\widehat{\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)}
. Using infinite products, one may find examples of discrete quantum groups with AP which are not weakly amenable. We make further remarks at the start of Section
4
.

Let us now briefly describe more of our results and explain how the paper is organised. In Section
2
we collect some background material on locally compact quantum groups and fix our notation.
In Section
3
we review several characterisations of the space
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
of left cb-multipliers of the Fourier algebra
\A(\mathbb{G})
and
its natural predual
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. By definition,
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is a (in general not closed) subalgebra of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, but it is also isomorphic to
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
, the space of a normal left module maps on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Any map in
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
restricts to
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and we provide a characterisation of the maps on
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
which so arise. This leads to a description of
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
as a quotient of the projective tensor product
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We were unable to locate this description in the literature, even for classical groups.

In Section
4
we define the Haagerup-Kraus approximation property for locally compact quantum groups, and survey some examples and counter-examples, making use of later results in the paper. We verify that AP passes to opposite and commutant quantum groups.
We compare our definition to the version of AP given by Kraus and Ruan, showing that they are equivalent. We show that
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
admits an involution linked to the antipode of
\mathbb{G}
, and to the fact that elements of
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
act boundedly on the Hilbert space
\LL^{2}(\mathbb{G})
. We finish the section by showing that AP is independent of the choice of working with left or right multipliers.

In Section
5
we discuss the relation of AP with weak amenability and coamenability.

Section
6
is devoted to the special case of discrete quantum groups. When
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
is discrete, we have the notion of a finitely-supported function leading to the algebra
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
. It suffices to work with
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
when considering AP, and we show further that the approximating net can be chosen to satisfy other properties. We introduce the central approximation property for discrete quantum groups and prove
that central AP is equivalent to AP in the unimodular case. Building on the work of Kraus-Ruan
[
42
]
and Crann
[
14
]
, we show that if a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
has AP then the von Neumann algebra
\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
has W
∗
OAP. We study the relation between AP of
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
and (strong) OAP of
\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold{\Gamma}})
or
W
∗
OAP of
\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold{\Gamma}})
. Finally, we introduce strengthenings of these concepts which take into account also the algebra
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
, and show that these are equivalent to AP even in the non-unimodular case.

In Section
7
we establish a number of permanence properties. We show that the AP is inherited by arbitrary closed quantum subgroups and by the duals
of double crossed products. In particular, the direct product of two quantum groups with AP also has AP. For discrete quantum groups we investigate the passage to free products and direct unions, again showing that AP is preserved.

Finally, in Section
8
we define the approximation property for rigid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-tensor categories and verify that the categorical AP is equivalent
to the central AP for discrete quantum groups. This implies in particular that the central AP is invariant under monoidal equivalence. We also relate these properties to the AP of the Drinfeld double.

We conclude with some general remarks on notation. If
\mathcal{A}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra we write
\M(\mathcal{A})
for its multiplier algebra.
For a map
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
, the symbol
\Phi^{\dagger}
stands for the map
\mathcal{A}\ni a\mapsto\Phi(a^{*})^{*}\in\mathcal{A}
.
If
\omega:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a linear functional we write
\overline{\omega}
for the linear functional given by
\overline{\omega}(x)=\overline{\omega(x^{*})}
.

We write
\odot
for the algebraic tensor product,
\otimes
for the tensor product of Hilbert spaces or the minimal tensor product of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras,

\check{\otimes}
for the injective tensor product of operator spaces and
\bar{\otimes}
for the spatial tensor product of von Neumann algebras.
We denote by
\chi
the flip map for tensor products of algebras, and use the symbol
\Sigma
for the flip map of Hilbert spaces.

We freely use the basic theory of operator spaces, following
[
23
]
, see also
[
54
,
55
]
for example. When
X,Y
are operator spaces,
\CB(X,Y)
denotes the space of completely bounded (CB) linear maps
X\rightarrow Y
. For dual operator spaces
X,Y
we write
\CB^{\sigma}(X,Y)

for the subset of
\CB(X,Y)
consisting of all maps which are weak
∗
-weak
∗
-continuous.
In the case
X=Y
we simply write
\CB(X)=\CB(X,X)
and
\CB^{\sigma}(X)=\CB^{\sigma}(X,X)
. If
\M
is a von Neumann algebra, then
\CB^{\sigma}(\M)
can be equipped with the stable point-weak
∗
-topology:
T_{i}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}T
with respect to this topology if and only if
(T_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})x\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}(T\otimes\textnormal{id})x
in the weak
∗
-topology, for any separable Hilbert space
\mathsf{H}
and
x\in\M\bar{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})
, see
[
32
]
. Whenever we have a left
\N
-module structure on an operator space
X
, the space of
left
\N
-module CB maps is denoted by
\prescript{}{\N}{\CB}(X)
. Similarly, if
X
is a right module or a bimodule, the corresponding spaces are denoted
by
\CB_{\M}(X)
and
\prescript{}{\N}{\CB}_{\M}(X)
, respectively. We denote the operator space projective tensor product by
\widehat{\otimes}
, and recall that
(X\widehat{\otimes}Y)^{*}=\CB(X,Y^{*})
completely isometrically. The canonical pairing between an operator space
X
and its dual
X^{*}
is denoted by
\langle\omega,x\rangle_{X^{*},X}

for
\omega\in X^{*},x\in X
, or simply
\langle\omega,x\rangle
if there is no risk of confusion.

For a n.s.f. weight
\theta
on a von Neumann algebra
\M
, we denote the GNS Hilbert space by
\mathsf{H}_{\theta}
, and we use the notation
\mathfrak{N}_{\theta}=\{x\in\M\,|\,\theta(x^{*}x)<+\infty\}
. We write
\Lambda_{\theta}\colon\mathfrak{N}_{\theta}\rightarrow\mathsf{H}_{\theta}
for the GNS map. Typically we then represent
\M
on
\mathsf{H}_{\theta}
and
identify
\M\subseteq\B(\mathsf{H}_{\theta})
.

2.
Preliminaries

Throughout the paper we will work in the setting of locally compact quantum groups introduced by Kustermans and Vaes
[
46
]
. In this section we recall some
fundamental constructions and results of the theory, more information can be found in
[
44
,
47
,
70
]
. For background on operator algebras and
operator spaces we refer to
[
10
,
23
,
65
]
.

By definition, a
locally compact quantum group

\mathbb{G}
is given by a von Neumann algebra
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
together with a normal unital
\star
-homomorphism
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
called
comultiplication
, satisfying
(\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
,
and
left
resp.
right

Haar integrals

\varphi
and
\psi
. These are normal, semifinite, faithful (n.s.f.) weights
on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
satisfying certain invariance conditions with respect to
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
. In general, the von Neumann algebra
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is non-commutative and will not be an algebra of functions on a measure space. Following this notational convention, the predual of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is denoted by
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
and the GNS Hilbert space of
\varphi
is denoted by
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
.

Every locally compact group
G
can be seen as a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
by taking
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})=\LL^{\infty}(G)
, the algebra of classes of measurable, bounded functions on
G
,
and letting
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
be the pullback of multiplication in
G
. The weights
\varphi,\psi
are given by integration with respect to left (right) Haar measure in this case.

Out of the axioms, one can construct a number of additional objects associated to a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
.
First of all, there is the
Kac-Takesaki operator

\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, which is a unitary operator on
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
defined via

(2.1)

((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x))\qquad(\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}),x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}).

It implements the comultiplication via
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)=\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.
Tomita-Takesaki theory yields two groups of
modular automorphisms

(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}},(\sigma^{\psi}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

and
modular conjugations

J_{\varphi},J_{\psi}
associated with the weights
\varphi,\psi
, respectively
[
65
]
. The left and right Haar integrals are linked by the
modular element

\delta_{\mathbb{G}}
, which is a strictly positive, self-adjoint operator affiliated with
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
.

In the theory of quantum groups, the role of the inverse operation is played by two maps: the
antipode

S_{\mathbb{G}}
and the
unitary antipode

R_{\mathbb{G}}
.
The antipode is in general an unbounded (densely defined) map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
such that

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{Dom}(S_{\mathbb{G}})\;\textnormal{ and }S_{\mathbb{G}}((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}\qquad(\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

The unitary antipode, on the other hand, is a bounded, normal,
\star
-preserving, antimultiplicative map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
satisfying
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}R_{\mathbb{G}}=\chi(R_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes R_{\mathbb{G}})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
. These maps are linked via
S_{\mathbb{G}}=R_{\mathbb{G}}\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-i/2}=\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-i/2}R_{\mathbb{G}}
, where
(\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is the group of
scaling automorphisms
of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. There is a self-adjoint, strictly positive (in general unbounded) operator
P_{\mathbb{G}}
acting on
\LL^{2}(\mathbb{G})
which implements scaling automorphisms via
\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(x)=P^{it}_{\mathbb{G}}xP^{-it}_{\mathbb{G}}\,(x\in\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),t\in\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore,
P_{\mathbb{G}}
is self-dual in the sense that
P_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=P_{\mathbb{G}}
. The left and right Haar integrals are unique up to a scalar, and we shall fix normalisations such that
\varphi=\psi\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}
. The scaling constant of
\mathbb{G}
will be denoted by
\nu_{\mathbb{G}}>0
. It is characterised via for example
\varphi\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}=\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{-t}\varphi
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

With any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
one can associate the
dual
locally compact quantum group
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
in such a way that the correspondence between
\mathbb{G}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
extends
Pontryagin duality. Furthermore, the Hilbert spaces
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}),\LL^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
can be identified in a canonical way,
and the Kac-Takesaki operators of
\mathbb{G}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
are linked via
\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=\chi(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})
.
If there is no risk of confusion we will simply write
\Delta
for
\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
,
\widehat{\Delta}
for
\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
, and similarly
R,S,\widehat{R},\widehat{S}
for the (unitary) antipode
of
\mathbb{G}
or
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
.
Using the canonical identification of
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
and
\LL^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
one obtains a number of useful formulae. Let us mention in particular that the right regular
representation
\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is given by
\mathrm{V}^{\mathbb{G}}=(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes J_{\widehat{\varphi}})\chi(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})^{*}(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes J_{\widehat{\varphi}})
.

We will also work with the weak
∗
-dense
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-subalgebra
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. It is defined as the norm-closure of the space
\{(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\,|\,\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\}
. After restriction, the comultiplication becomes a non-degenerate
\star
-homomorphism
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
.
Similarly one defines
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and then
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.
Using the comultiplication of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, we define a Banach algebra structure on
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
via
\omega\star\nu=(\omega\otimes\nu)\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}
for
\omega,\nu\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
. As
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is the dual of
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, we have a canonical
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
-bimodule structure on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, which is given by
\omega\star x=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)
and
x\star\omega=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{G}}(x)
. Treating
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
as the predual of the von Neumann algebra
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
gives, as usual, an
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
-bimodule structure on
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
defined via
x\omega=\omega(\cdot\,x),\omega x=\omega(x\,\cdot)
for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
.

Let us introduce the map
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
by
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega)=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
, and similarly for
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. Using this we define the
Fourier algebra of
\mathbb{G}
as
\A(\mathbb{G})=\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. One can check that
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
is multiplicative, so that
\A(\mathbb{G})
is a dense subalgebra of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
.
As
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
is also injective, we can define an operator space structure on
\A(\mathbb{G})
by imposing the condition that
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\A(\mathbb{G})
is
completely isometric.

In the text, we will use certain subspaces of
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, which consist of functionals having nice additional properties. Firstly, let us introduce

(2.2)

\begin{split}\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})&=\{\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,\exists_{\theta\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})}\,\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega)^{*}=\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\theta)\}\\
&=\{\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,\exists_{\theta\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})}\,\forall_{x\in\operatorname{Dom}(S_{\mathbb{G}})}\,\overline{\omega}(S_{\mathbb{G}}(x))=\theta(x)\}.\end{split}

For a given
\omega\in\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})
, the functional
\theta
is characterised uniquely by any of the properties in (
2.2
), hence we can write
\theta=\omega^{\sharp}
. The mapping
\omega\mapsto\omega^{\sharp}
, and the restriction of the multiplication from
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, turn
\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})
into a
\star
-algebra. The second subspace we will use is

(2.3)

\mathscr{J}=\{\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,\exists_{M>0}\,\forall_{x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}}\,|\omega(x^{*})|\leq M\|\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\|\}.

This subspace appears in the construction of the left Haar integral
\widehat{\varphi}
for
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. Indeed, for
\omega\in\mathscr{J}
we have

\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}\quad\textnormal{ and }\quad\forall_{x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}}\,\langle\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\,|\,\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\rangle=\omega(x^{*}).

In a couple of places we will need the following result, which says that there are many functionals with desirable properties.

Lemma 2.1
.

The subspace

\begin{split}\mathscr{J}_{0}=\{\omega\in\mathscr{J}\cap\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,&\overline{\omega},\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\in\mathscr{J}\cap\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})\textnormal{ and }f\colon\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto(\omega\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{it})\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\\
&\textnormal{extends to an entire function such that }\forall_{z\in\mathbb{C}}\,f(z)\in\mathscr{J}\cap\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})\}\end{split}

is dense in
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, and
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\mathscr{J}_{0})
forms a
\sigma\textsc{-sot}^{*}\times\|\cdot\|
core for
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}
.

Proof.

Our approach is standard, compare for example
[
39
, Lemma 14.5]
for a similar result. Therefore we only give a sketch of the argument.

According to
[
47
, Proposition 2.6]
, the space
\mathscr{J}^{\sharp}=\{\omega\in\mathscr{J}\cap\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})\,|\,\omega^{\sharp}\in\mathscr{J}\}
is dense in
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
and
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\mathscr{J}^{\sharp})
is a
\sigma\textsc{-sot}^{*}\times\|\cdot\|
core of
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}
. Let us introduce three mollifier operations: for
n\in\mathbb{N}
let

\begin{split}M^{\varphi}_{n}&\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\ni\omega\mapsto\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-nt^{2}}\omega\circ\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\operatorname{d}\!t\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}),\\
M^{\tau}_{n}&\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\ni\omega\mapsto\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ns^{2}}\omega\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\operatorname{d}\!s\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}),\\
M^{\delta}_{n}&\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\ni\omega\mapsto\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-np^{2}}\omega\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\operatorname{d}\!p\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}).\end{split}

Next, let
\omega_{n}=M^{\tau}_{n}\circ M^{\varphi}_{n}\circ M^{\delta}_{n}(\omega)
and set
\mathscr{J}_{00}=\lin\{\omega_{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N},\omega\in\mathscr{J}^{\sharp}\}
.
It suffices to show that
\mathscr{J}_{00}
is dense in
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, that
\mathscr{J}_{00}\subseteq\mathscr{J}_{0}
, and that
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\mathscr{J}_{00})
forms a
\sigma\textsc{-sot}^{*}\times\|\cdot\|
core for
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}
.

Choose
n\in\mathbb{N},\omega\in\mathscr{J}^{\sharp},x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi},y\in\Dom(S_{\mathbb{G}})
. It is elementary to check that
\omega_{n}\in\mathscr{J}\cap\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})
and
\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto(\omega_{n}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{it})\circ\tau_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
extends to an entire function with the desired property. Since

\begin{split}&\quad\;|\overline{\omega_{n}}(x^{*})|=|\omega_{n}(x)|=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\omega(\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}(x))\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr|\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\bigl|\omega\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}(x)\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr|\\
&\leq(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|\Lambda_{\varphi}\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}(x^{*})\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{-ip}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|J_{\varphi}\nabla_{\varphi}^{1/2}\Lambda_{\varphi}\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}(x)\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|\Lambda_{\varphi}\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n((t+i/2)^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{p/2}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}(x)\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&\leq(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|e^{-n((t+i/2)^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}|\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{p/2-s/2}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\|\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\|,\end{split}

where
\nu_{\mathbb{G}}
is the scaling constant of
\mathbb{G}
, we have
\overline{\omega_{n}}\in\mathscr{J}
. Here we used
\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip})=\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{itp}\delta^{ip}
and
\varphi\circ\tau_{s}=\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{-s}\varphi
. It is automatic that
\overline{\omega_{n}}\in\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})
. Indeed,

\begin{split}\overline{\overline{\omega_{n}}}(S_{\mathbb{G}}(y))=\omega_{n}(S_{\mathbb{G}}(y))&=\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ns^{2}}M^{\varphi}_{n}\circ M^{\delta}_{n}(\omega)(\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-i/2}(y))\operatorname{d}\!s\\
&=\bigl(\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-n(s+i/2)^{2}}M^{\varphi}_{n}\circ M^{\delta}_{n}(\omega)\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}\operatorname{d}\!s\bigr)(y).\end{split}

The above calculation shows also that
\overline{\omega_{n}}^{\sharp}=\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-n(s+i/2)^{2}}M^{\varphi}_{n}\circ M^{\delta}_{n}(\omega)\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}\operatorname{d}\!s
. Moreover we have
\overline{\omega_{n}}^{\sharp}\in\mathscr{J}
, which is a consequence of the following calculation:

\begin{split}&\quad\;|\overline{\omega_{n}}^{\sharp}(x^{*})|=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+(s+i/2)^{2}+p^{2})}\omega\bigl(\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau_{s}^{\mathbb{G}}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}(x^{*})\bigr)\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr|\\
&\leq(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|\Lambda_{\varphi}\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+(s-i/2)^{2}+p^{2})}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}(x)\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{-ip}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|\Lambda_{\psi}\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+(s-i/2)^{2}+(p+i/2)^{2})}\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}(x)\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|\Lambda_{\psi}\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+(s-i/2)^{2}+(p+i/2)^{2})}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{-it}\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}(x)\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{i(t+p)}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&=\!(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|J_{\psi}\nabla_{\psi}^{1/2}\Lambda_{\psi}\bigl(\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!e^{-n(t^{2}+(s+i/2)^{2}+(p-i/2)^{2})}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{-i(t+p)}\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ R_{\mathbb{G}}(x^{*})\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{it}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&=\!(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl\|\Lambda_{\psi}\!\bigl(\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\!\!e^{-n((t+i/2)^{2}\!+(s+i/2)^{2}\!+(p-i/2)^{2})}\!\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{-p/2}\!\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{-i(t+p)}\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\!\circ\!\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\!\circ\!R_{\mathbb{G}}(x^{*})\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{it}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\bigr\|\\
&\leq(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|e^{-n((t+i/2)^{2}+(s+i/2)^{2}+(p-i/2)^{2})}|\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{s/2-t/2-p/2}\|\Lambda_{\psi}(R_{\mathbb{G}}(x^{*}))\|\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\|\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\|\bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|e^{-n((t+i/2)^{2}+(s+i/2)^{2}+(p-i/2)^{2})}|\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{s/2-t/2-p/2}\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\bigr)\|\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\|,\end{split}

where we used
\Lambda_{\varphi}(z)=\Lambda_{\psi}(z\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{1/2})
for sufficiently good operators
z
,
\sigma^{\psi}_{t}=\operatorname{Ad}(\delta^{it}_{\mathbb{G}})\circ\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}
,
\psi\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}=\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{-s}\psi
and
\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip})=\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{itp}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip}
. We are left to argue that
\mathscr{J}_{00}
is dense and that
\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\mathscr{J}_{00})
is a
\textsc{sot}^{*}\times\|\cdot\|
core for
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}
. It follows from

\omega_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\omega,\quad\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega_{n}))\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))

for
\omega\in\mathscr{J}^{\sharp}
. The first claim is standard (see e.g.
[
43
, Proposition 2.25]
where a similar method is used), the second one can be seen as follows: first, observe that for
t,s,p\in\mathbb{R}
we have using
(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{-t})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it})
(see equation (
4.5
)) and
(\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\otimes\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{s})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
that
(\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-t}\otimes\sigma^{\widehat{\varphi}}_{-t})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=(\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
and

\begin{split}&\quad\;\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}((\omega\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip})\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ\sigma^{\varphi}_{t})=((\omega\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip})\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{-t})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it})\bigr)\\
&=(\omega\otimes\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{-t-s})\bigl((\delta^{ip}_{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it}=(\omega\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{-p}\otimes\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{-t-s}\circ\sigma^{\widehat{\varphi}}_{-p})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it}\\
&=(\omega\otimes\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{p-t-s}\circ\sigma^{\widehat{\varphi}}_{-p})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it}=\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{p-t-s}\circ\sigma^{\widehat{\varphi}}_{-p}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it}.\end{split}

Hence

\begin{split}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega_{n}))&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}((\omega\delta_{\mathbb{G}}^{ip})\circ\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}\circ\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}))\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}\bigl(\tau^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{p-t-s}\circ\sigma^{\widehat{\varphi}}_{-p}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{it}\bigr)\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p\\
&=(\tfrac{n}{\pi})^{3/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-n(t^{2}+s^{2}+p^{2})}\nu_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{s/2-p/2}J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{-it}J_{\widehat{\varphi}}P^{i(p-t-s)}\nabla_{\widehat{\varphi}}^{-ip}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))\operatorname{d}\!t\operatorname{d}\!s\operatorname{d}\!p,\end{split}

where
P
is the operator implementing the scaling group via
P^{it}\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\nu_{\mathbb{G}}^{t/2}\Lambda_{\varphi}(\tau^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}(x))
. Convergence
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega_{n}))\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda_{\mathbb{G}}(\omega))
follows now using a standard argument.
∎

3.
Completely bounded multipliers

Unless stated otherwise, in this section
\mathbb{G}
is an arbitrary locally compact quantum group.

3.1.
Definitions and fundamental facts

We start by discussing the notions of (left/right) centralisers and multipliers. In the main part of the text we will focus on the left version of these
objects – this is simply a matter of choice, compare Proposition
4.14
.

Following
[
34
]
, we say that a linear map
T\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is a
left (respectively, right) centraliser
if

T(\omega\star\omega^{\prime})=T(\omega)\star\omega^{\prime}\quad\bigl(\textnormal{respectively}\;T(\omega\star\omega^{\prime})=\omega\star T(\omega^{\prime})\bigr)\qquad(\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

We denote by
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
the space of completely bounded left centralisers. Together with the completely bounded norm and composition as product,
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
becomes a Banach algebra. Similarly,
C^{r}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
stands for the space of completely bounded right centralisers, where now it is natural to use the opposite composition as product. We equip these spaces with an operator space structure by requiring that the embeddings
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})),C^{r}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\hookrightarrow\CB(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
are completely isometric; both then become completely contractive Banach algebras.

An operator
b\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is said to be a
completely bounded left multiplier
if
b\A(\mathbb{G})\subseteq\A(\mathbb{G})
and the associated map

\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\quad\text{satisfying}\quad b\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}(\omega))\qquad(\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))

is completely bounded. As
\widehat{\lambda}
is injective, this definition makes sense. We follow here the notation of
[
13
]
; sometimes the notation
m^{l}_{b}=\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}
is used instead. As
\widehat{\lambda}
is multiplicative, for any completely bounded left multiplier
b
we have that
\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. We write
\Theta^{l}(b)=(\Theta^{l}(b)_{*})^{*}
, and denote the space of CB left multipliers by
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Any Fourier algebra element
\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\in\A(\mathbb{G})
is a CB left multiplier with
\Theta^{l}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega))_{*}\in\CB(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
being the left multiplication by
\omega
and
\Theta^{l}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega))=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
. Moreover, it holds that
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
, see
[
17
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Conversely, if
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is a left centraliser, then its Banach space dual
T^{*}
is a normal CB map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
which is a left
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
-module homomorphism, i.e.
T^{*}\in\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))}
. Then, by
[
35
, Corollary 4.4]
, there exists a unique CB left multiplier
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
satisfying
\Theta^{l}(b)=T^{*}
, that is,
\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}=T
. These constructions are mutually inverse, and so the map
\Theta^{l}(\cdot)_{*}:\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\rightarrow C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is bijective. We define the operator space structure on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
so that these spaces become completely isometric.

The above notions have right counterparts. Recalling that
\mathrm{V}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is the right Kac-Takesaki operator, let us introduce the map
\widehat{\rho}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\ni\omega\mapsto(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{V}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}
. Its image
\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
should be thought of as a right analogue of the Fourier algebra
\A(\mathbb{G})=\widehat{\lambda}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. An operator
b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}
is called a
completely bounded right multiplier
if
\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))b^{\prime}\in\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
and the associated map
\Theta^{r}(b^{\prime})_{*}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is CB. Similarly as in the left version, we write
\Theta^{r}(b^{\prime})\in\CB^{\sigma}_{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
for
(\Theta^{r}(b^{\prime})_{*})^{*}
and
\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
for the space of CB right multipliers. Any CB right centraliser
T\in C^{r}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is associated to a unique CB right multiplier
b^{\prime}\in\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
via
T=\Theta^{r}(b^{\prime})_{*}
and this assignment is bijective. We similarly define an operator space structure on
\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
to make it completely isometric with
C^{r}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.

We will write e.g.
\|b\|_{cb}=\|\Theta^{l}(b)\|_{cb}
for
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Observe that
b\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(b)_{*}(\omega))
for each
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
if and only if
(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(\Theta^{l}(b)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})
, and from this, it follows that
\|b\|\leq\|b\|_{cb}
. Similarly we have
\|b^{\prime}\|\leq\|b^{\prime}\|_{cb}
for
b^{\prime}\in\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
.

As a consequence of the above discussion, we have a commutative diagram

(3.1)

\textstyle{C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}
\textstyle{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\A(\mathbb{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The two diagonal maps to
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
are the canonical inclusions, as is the map
\A(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, while the vertical map
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is given by left multiplication. A simple calculation shows that this diagram indeed commutes. We obtain an immediate corollary: the map
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is injective, equivalently, if
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
with
\omega\star\omega^{\prime}=0
for all
\omega^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, then
\omega=0
.

There is a canonical way of moving between left and right CB multipliers using the extension of the unitary antipode
\widehat{R}
of
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
. Recall that it is implemented
via
\widehat{R}=J_{\varphi}(\cdot)^{*}J_{\varphi}
, and let us denote its canonical extension to a bounded linear map on
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
by
{\widehat{R}}^{\sim}=J_{\varphi}(\cdot)^{*}J_{\varphi}
. The following result will be used in Proposition
4.14
to show that it does not matter if we use left CB multipliers, or right CB multipliers, when we introduce the approximation property (AP), see Definition
4.1
below.

Lemma 3.1
.

For
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
we have
\widehat{\rho}(\omega)=\widehat{R}^{\sim}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega\circ\widehat{R}))
. Furthermore,
\widehat{R}^{\sim}(\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G})))=\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
and for
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
we have
\Theta^{r}(\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a))=\widehat{R}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\circ\widehat{R}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathrm{V}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(J_{\varphi}\otimes J_{\varphi})\chi(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})^{*}(J_{\varphi}\otimes J_{\varphi})
, see
[
47
, Proposition 2.15]
. It follows that

(3.2)

\widehat{\rho}(\omega)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{V}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=\widehat{R}^{\sim}\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega\circ\widehat{R})(\chi(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}))\bigr)=\widehat{R}^{\sim}\bigl((\omega\circ\widehat{R}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\bigr)=\widehat{R}^{\sim}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega\circ\widehat{R})).

Next, take
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
and
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We have
\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a)=J_{\varphi}a^{*}J_{\varphi}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}
, so by (
3.2
),

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}(\omega)\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a)

\displaystyle=\widehat{R}^{\sim}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega\circ\widehat{R}))\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a)=\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a\widehat{\lambda}(\omega\circ\widehat{R}))

\displaystyle=\widehat{R}^{\sim}\bigl(\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\omega\circ\widehat{R}))\bigr)=\widehat{\rho}\bigl(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\omega\circ\widehat{R})\circ\widehat{R}\bigr).

Hence
\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a)\in\M^{r}_{cb}(a)
with
\Theta^{r}(\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a))=\widehat{R}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\circ\widehat{R}
; this map is indeed CB, compare Lemma
4.8
. We have shown that
\widehat{R}^{\sim}(\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G})))\subseteq\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
; the converse inclusion is analogous.
∎

We finish by recording a known result for which we have not found a convenient reference.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. There is
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Theta^{l}(b)(\mathbbm{1})=\beta\mathbbm{1}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for
T\in\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))}
there is
\beta
with
T(\mathbbm{1})=\beta\mathbbm{1}
. By definition,
\Delta\circ T=(T\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta
and so
\Delta(T(\mathbbm{1}))=T(\mathbbm{1})\otimes\mathbbm{1}
. By
[
18
, Theorem 2.1]
(and references therein) it follows that
T(\mathbbm{1})\in\mathbb{C}\mathbbm{1}
, as required.
∎

3.2.
Predual

Since the inclusion
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\hookrightarrow\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is bounded (actually, contractive), we can consider the restriction of the Banach space adjoint of this map, giving a map
\alpha^{l}\colon\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}
. Let us define the space
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
as the closure of the image of
\alpha^{l}
, so that

Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))=\overline{\alpha^{l}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}))}\subseteq\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}.

According to
[
34
, Theorem 3.4]
, the space
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is a predual of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, i.e. we have

Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}\cong\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))

completely isometrically. Whenever we speak about the weak
∗
-topology on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
we will have in mind this particular choice of predual – to the best of our knowledge, uniqueness of predual of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is unknown.

Similarly, we can restrict functionals in
\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
to
\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
via map
\alpha^{r}
, and after taking the closure obtain the predual
Q^{r}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))\subseteq\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))^{*}
. From now on, we will restrict our discussion to the “left” setting.

Proposition 3.3
.

\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is a dual Banach algebra, that is, the multiplication of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is separately weak
∗
-continuous.

Proof.

We turn
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}
into a
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
-bimodule in the usual way. Let
a,b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and
f\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
. Writing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the pairing between
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G})
and
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, or between
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle ab,\alpha^{l}(f)\rangle=\langle ab,f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle a,bf\rangle=\langle a,\alpha^{l}(bf)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle b,fa\rangle=\langle b,\alpha^{l}(fa)\rangle.

This calculation shows that
b\cdot\alpha^{l}(f)=\alpha^{l}(bf)
and
\alpha^{l}(f)\cdot a=\alpha^{l}(fa)
, so by continuity, it follows that
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is a closed submodule of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}
. It is now standard, see
[
60
, Proposition 1.2]
for example, that the product is separately weak
∗
-continuous in
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.
∎

Our next goal is to obtain a characterisation of functionals in
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. We will do this by obtaining an alternative description of the weak
∗
-topology on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. In the process, we also discuss CB maps on the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
which are associated to left centralisers.

To start, we observe that the adjoint
T^{*}
of a CB left centraliser
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
restricts to a CB map on
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Indeed, we can write
T^{*}=\Theta^{l}(a)
for some
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
and then the claim follows from the equality
(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(T^{*}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
and density of
\A(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
in
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We seek a characterisation of which CB maps on
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
occur in this way as restrictions of duals to left CB centralisers, in terms of a property similar to the characterisation
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\cong\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.

In the following statement, recall that
(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\widehat{\Delta})
is bisimplifiable, and so elements of the form
\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)
, for
a,b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, form a linearly dense subset of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Hence the left-hand-side of (
3.3
) is contained in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, while the right-hand-side is
in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We also recall that, by Kaplansky density,
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is (completely) isometrically a subspace of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
L\in\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
be such that

(3.3)

(L\otimes\textnormal{id})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))=\widehat{\Delta}(L(a))(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\qquad(a,b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

Embedding
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
into the duals of
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
in the usual way, we have that
L^{*}
maps
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
to itself, and the resulting restriction
T\in\CB(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is a left centraliser. Furthermore
T^{*}\in\CB(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
restricts to
L
, so consequently
L\in\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.

Proof.

As
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is an essential
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
-module, by Cohen–Hewitt factorisation, given
\omega_{2}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
there are
\omega_{3}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
with
\omega_{2}=b\omega_{3}
.
Then, for
\omega_{1}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
we have

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle L^{*}(\omega_{1}\star\omega_{2}),a\rangle_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*},\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}=\langle\widehat{\Delta}(L(a)),\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}\\
&=\langle\widehat{\Delta}(L(a))(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b),\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{3}\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}\\
&=\langle(L\otimes\textnormal{id})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)),\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{3}\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}\\
&=\langle L^{*}(\omega_{1})\otimes\omega_{3},\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\rangle_{(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))^{*},\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}\\
&=\langle L^{*}(\omega_{1}),(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{3})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))\rangle_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*},\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}\\
&=\langle L^{*}(\omega_{1}),(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{2})\widehat{\Delta}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*},\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}\\
&=\langle L^{*}(\omega_{1})\star\omega_{2},a\rangle_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*},\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}.\end{split}

It follows that
L^{*}(\omega_{1}\star\omega_{2})=L^{*}(\omega_{1})\star\omega_{2}
in
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
. As
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
is an ideal in
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*}
(
[
46
, Proof of Proposition 8.3]
), this shows that
L^{*}(\omega_{1}\star\omega_{2})\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and as products have dense linear span in
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
(
[
13
, Section 3]
), we conclude that
L^{*}
restricts to a map on
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, say
T\in\CB(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. Then
T(\omega_{1}\star\omega_{2})=T(\omega_{1})\star\omega_{2}
for all
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
, and so
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. We finally calculate that, for
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
,

\displaystyle\langle T^{*}(a),\omega\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}

\displaystyle=\langle a,T(\omega)\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}=\langle L^{*}(\omega),a\rangle_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{*},\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}=\langle L(a),\omega\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})},

and so
T^{*}
restricts to
L
, as required.
∎

We can now characterise what it means for an operator in
\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
to be a centraliser. Condition (
2
) in the following proposition should be thought of as a
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
variant of what it means to be a left
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
-module homomorphism.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
L\in\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
the following are equivalent:

(1)

there is
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
such that
T^{*}
restricts to
L
;

(2)

(L\otimes\textnormal{id})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))=\widehat{\Delta}(L(a))(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)
for each
a,b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
;

Furthermore, the restriction map
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\cong\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is a complete isometry; in particular, there is a bijection between
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
and the space of all maps
L\in\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
satisfying (
2
).

Proof.

If (
1
) holds then
\widehat{\Delta}T^{*}=(T^{*}\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
and so certainly the condition in (
2
) will hold for
T^{*}
and hence also for
L
. Conversely, suppose that (
2
) holds. Then due to Lemma
3.4
we know that
L^{*}
restricts to a map
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
such that
T^{*}
restricts to
L
, showing (
1
).

The restriction map
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is clearly a complete contraction. With
T,L
as above, this restriction map is given by
T^{*}\mapsto L
, and as
T
is the restriction of
L^{*}
and
L\mapsto L^{*},T\mapsto T^{*}
are completely isometric, it follows that
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is a complete isometry.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Equip the space
\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
with the weak
∗
-topology arising from the canonical predual
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. The restriction map

C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\cong\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))

is a complete isometry which has weak
∗
-closed image.

Proof.

Proposition
3.5
shows that the restriction map
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}{\CB}^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is a complete isometry. Let
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
be a net in
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
such that the image of the net
(T_{i}^{*})_{i\in I}
in
\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
converges weak
∗
to
L\in\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. Let
a,b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, and note that

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{2})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We now calculate that

\displaystyle\quad\;\langle\widehat{\Delta}(L(a))(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b),\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}\rangle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle T_{i}^{*}(a),\omega_{1}\star(b\omega_{2})\rangle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle a,T_{i}(\omega_{1})\star(b\omega_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b),T_{i}(\omega_{1})\otimes\omega_{2}\rangle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle T_{i}^{*}\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{2})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))\big),\omega_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle L\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{2})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))\big),\omega_{1}\rangle=\langle(L\otimes\textnormal{id})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)),\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}\rangle.

All the above pairings are between a von Neumann algebra and its predual. It follows that we have
\widehat{\Delta}(L(a))(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)=(L\otimes\textnormal{id})(\widehat{\Delta}(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b))
in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. By Lemma
3.4
,
L^{*}
restricts to
T\in C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
such that
T^{*}
restricts back to give
L
. That is,
T_{i}^{*}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}T^{*}
weak
∗
in
\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
, as required.
∎

We now wish to show that the resulting weak
∗
-topology on
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
given by Proposition
3.6
agrees with the weak
∗
-topology on
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\cong\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
given by the predual
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. In the following, for a Banach space
E
, we denote by
\kappa_{E}\colon E\rightarrow E^{**}
the canonical map to the bidual.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
E,F
be Banach spaces, and let
\alpha:E^{*}\rightarrow F^{*}
be a bounded linear map. Let
D\subseteq F
be a subset with dense linear span. Then
\alpha
is weak
∗
-weak
∗
-continuous if and only if
\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}(D)\subseteq\kappa_{E}(E)
. In this case, and when further
\alpha
is a bijection, the resulting preadjoint
\alpha_{*}:F\rightarrow E
is also an isomorphism of Banach spaces and
\alpha
is a weak
∗
-weak
∗
-homeomorphism.

Proof.

If
\alpha
is weak
∗
-continuous, then there is a preadjoint operator
\alpha_{*}:F\rightarrow E
with
(\alpha_{*})^{*}=\alpha
, and so
\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}(D)=(\alpha_{*})^{**}\kappa_{F}(D)=\kappa_{E}\alpha_{*}(D)\subseteq\kappa_{E}(E)
, as claimed. Conversely, if
\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}(D)\subseteq\kappa_{E}(E)
then by norm density of
\lin D
in
F
, and norm continuity of
\alpha^{*}
, we have that
\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}(F)\subseteq\kappa_{E}(E)
. We could now directly apply
[
16
, Lemma 10.1]
, but let us give the argument. There is a linear map
T:F\rightarrow E
with
\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}(x)=\kappa_{E}(T(x))
for each
x\in F
. As
\kappa_{E},\kappa_{F}
are isometries,
T
is bounded with
\|T\|\leq\|\alpha^{*}\|=\|\alpha\|
. Then for
x\in F,\mu\in E^{*}
,

\langle T^{*}(\mu),x\rangle=\langle\mu,T(x)\rangle=\langle\kappa_{E}(T(x)),\mu\rangle=\langle\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}(x),\mu\rangle=\langle\alpha(\mu),x\rangle.

Hence
T^{*}=\alpha
and so
\alpha
is weak
∗
-continuous, with preadjoint
T
.

When
\alpha
is a bijection, by the Open Mapping Theorem, it is an isomorphism. Thus also
\alpha^{*}
is an isomorphism, and so as
\alpha^{*}\kappa_{F}=\kappa_{E}\alpha_{*}
it follows that
\alpha_{*}
is bounded below and so has closed image. If
\mu\in(\alpha_{*}(F))^{\perp}
then
0=\langle\mu,\alpha_{*}(x)\rangle=\langle\alpha(\mu),x\rangle
for all
x,\mu
and so
\alpha(\mu)=0
so
\mu=0
. Hence
\alpha_{*}
is a surjection, and so an isomorphism.
∎

Theorem 3.8
.

The weak
∗
-topology on
C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
given by the embedding into
\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
agrees with the weak
∗
-topology on
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
given by
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

Proof.

We use Lemma
3.7
. Set
E=Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. To avoid confusion, for this proof only, we shall write
\theta:C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\CB(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
for the complete isometry
T\mapsto T^{*}|_{\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}
, given by Proposition
3.6
. As the image of
\theta
is weak
∗
-closed, it has canonical predual
F
which is a quotient of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Let
\pi:\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow F
be the quotient map. We hence corestrict
\theta
to give an isomorphism
\theta:C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow F^{*}
. Let
\alpha_{0}:E^{*}=\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\rightarrow C^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
be the canonical bijection, and set
\alpha=\theta\circ\alpha_{0}:E^{*}\rightarrow F^{*}
.

Given
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
set
T=\alpha_{0}(a)
, so by definition,
a\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\widehat{\lambda}(T(\omega))
for each
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Equivalently,
(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(T^{*}\otimes\textnormal{id})({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})
. Given
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),f\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, set
u=\pi((\textnormal{id}\otimes f)({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\otimes\omega)\in F
, and calculate that

\displaystyle\ \langle\kappa_{E}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)f),a\rangle_{E^{**},E^{*}}=\langle a,\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)f\rangle_{E^{*},E}=\langle a\widehat{\lambda}(\omega),f\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})}=\langle(T^{*}\otimes\textnormal{id})({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}),\omega\otimes f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T^{*}\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes f)({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\big),\omega\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}=\langle\theta(T)\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes f)({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\big),\omega\rangle_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}=\langle\alpha(a),u\rangle_{F^{*},F}.

It follows that
\alpha^{*}(\kappa_{F}(u))=\kappa_{E}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)f)\in\kappa_{E}(E)
. As the linear span of such elements
u
is dense in
F
, the conditions of the lemma are verified, and the result follows.
∎

Using this result we can characterise functionals in the predual space
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. In the following, we work with infinite matrices with entries in operator spaces, see
[
23
, Chapter 10]
.

Proposition 3.9
.

For any Hilbert space
\mathsf{H}
and
x\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H}),\,\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
, the bounded linear functional

\Omega_{x,\omega}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\ni a\mapsto\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle\in\mathbb{C}.

belongs to
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Furthermore, all functionals in
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
are of this form for some separable Hilbert space.

This result was recorded without proof in
[
13
, Proposition 3.2]
. In the classical context of locally compact groups, an analogous result was proved by Haagerup and Kraus in
[
32
, Proposition 1.5]
. For the convenience of the reader, we give a proof using Theorem
3.8
.

Proof.

We first show that
\Omega_{x,\omega}
is a member of
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. As
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\subseteq\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))^{*}
is norm closed, by first approximating
x,\omega
by sums of elementary tensors, and then collapsing the pairing between
\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
and
\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
, we reduce the problem to the case when
\mathsf{H}=\mathbb{C}
, when
x=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\theta){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
for some
\theta\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, and when
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. We then calculate

\langle\Theta^{l}(a)\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\theta){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr),\omega\rangle=\langle\widehat{\lambda}(\omega\circ\Theta^{l}(a)),\theta\rangle=\langle a\widehat{\lambda}(\omega),\theta\rangle=\langle a,\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\theta\rangle\quad(a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))),

which shows that
\Omega_{x,\omega}=\alpha^{l}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\theta)\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, as required.

Now, take any functional in
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, which by Theorem
3.8
is represented by some element
\rho\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
(note that the projective operator space tensor product is symmetric). By
[
23
, Theorem 10.2.1]
, we can find infinite matrices

\alpha\in\M_{1,\infty\times\infty},\;\beta\in\mathsf{K}_{\infty}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})),\;\gamma\in\mathsf{K}_{\infty}(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})),\;\alpha^{\prime}\in\M_{\infty\times\infty,1}

such that
\rho=\alpha(\beta\otimes\gamma)\alpha^{\prime}
(for the introduction to infinite matrices with entries in an operator space, see
[
23
, Sections 10.1, 10.2]
). Writing
\alpha=[\alpha_{1,(i,j)}]_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
etc., this means that

(3.4)

\langle\Theta^{l}(a),\rho\rangle=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{1,(i,j)}\langle\Theta^{l}(a)(\gamma_{j,l}),\beta_{i,k}\rangle\alpha^{\prime}_{(k,l),1}\qquad(a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))).

Let
\mathsf{H}
be an infinite dimensional, separable Hilbert space with orthonormal basis
\{e_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and let
e_{i,j}\,(i,j\in\mathbb{N})
be the corresponding rank one operators. Write
T(\mathsf{H})
for the operator space of trace class operators, identified in a completely isometric way with
\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\|[e_{j,i}]_{i,j=1}^{n}\|_{\M_{n}(T(\mathsf{H}))}=1
. Indeed, the matrix
[e_{j,i}]_{i,j=1}^{n}
corresponds to the map
E_{n}\in\CB(\B(\mathsf{H}),\M_{n})
given by
E_{n}(x)=[\Tr(e_{j,i}x)]_{i,j=1}^{n}
. If we denote by
V_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathsf{H}
the canonical inclusion associated with the choice of basis, one easily sees that
E_{n}(x)=V_{n}^{*}xV_{n}\,(x\in\B(\mathsf{H}))
and
\|E_{n}\|_{cb}=1
follows. Consequently
[e_{j,i}]_{i,j=1}^{\infty}
is a well defined matrix in
\M_{\infty}(T(\mathsf{H}))
. Finally, define

\omega=\alpha(\beta\otimes[e_{j,i}]_{i,j=1}^{\infty})\alpha^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}T(\mathsf{H})=\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}.

A choice of basis gives us an isomorphism
\mathsf{H}\cong\ell^{2}
and consequently we can consider
\gamma
as an element of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
(
[
23
, Equation 10.1.2]
). Finally, using equation (
3.4
) we can show that the functional associated to
\rho
is of the form
\Omega_{\gamma,\omega}
. Indeed, we have

\begin{split}\langle a,\Omega_{\gamma,\omega}\rangle=\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\gamma,\omega\rangle&=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{1,(i,j)}\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\gamma,\beta_{i,k}\otimes e_{l,j}\rangle\alpha^{\prime}_{(k,l),1}\\
&=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{1,(i,j)}\langle\Theta^{l}(a)(\gamma_{j,l}),\beta_{i,k}\rangle\alpha^{\prime}_{(k,l),1}\end{split}

for any
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.
∎

3.3.
Viewing multipliers as bimodule maps

In this section we provide another way of looking at CB multipliers and the associated weak
∗
-topology which will be useful in later considerations.

Let us first introduce some terminology. As usual, let
\CB^{\sigma}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
be the space of normal CB maps on
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
. Observe
\CB^{\sigma}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))\cong\CB(\mathcal{K}(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})),\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
. Indeed, any normal CB map on
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
can be restricted to
\mathcal{K}(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
without changing its norm, and conversely, since
\mathcal{K}(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))^{**}\simeq\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
, any CB map
\mathcal{K}(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))\rightarrow\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
uniquely extends to a normal CB map on
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
.
\CB^{\sigma}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
is an operator space which is equipped with the weak
∗
-topology given by the predual
\mathcal{K}(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))\widehat{\otimes}\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))_{*}
. Via left and right multiplication,
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
becomes a
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}
-bimodule, hence we can consider normal CB bimodule maps on
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
. We can also look at those maps which leave
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\subseteq\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
globally invariant. We will denote the set of CB normal
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}
-bimodule maps on
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
which leave
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
globally invariant by
\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}}{\CB}_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}}^{\sigma,\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
. One easily checks that this space is weak
∗
-closed
in
\CB^{\sigma}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
, hence it naturally inherits an operator space structure and a weak
∗
-topology.

According to
[
35
, Theorem 4.5]
(and
[
18
, Proposition 3.3]
for the left version), for any
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
there exists a unique map
\Phi(a)\in\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}}{\CB}_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}}^{\sigma,\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
which extends
\Theta^{l}(a)\in\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
. This map satisfies

\mathbbm{1}\otimes\Phi(a)(x)={\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigl((\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\bigr){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}\qquad(x\in\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))).

Furthermore, the resulting map

(3.5)

\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\ni a\mapsto\Phi(a)\in\prescript{}{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}}{\CB}_{\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})^{\prime}}^{\sigma,\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})}(\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))

is a completely isometric isomorphism which is additionally a weak
∗
-homeomorphism (
[
18
, Theorem 6.2]
).

When
a
arises from an element of the Fourier algebra,
\Phi(a)
takes a special form.

Lemma 3.10
.

For
\omega\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
let
a=\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\in\A(\mathbb{G})
, so that
\Theta^{l}(a)=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
. The associated map
\Phi(a)
is

\Phi(a)\colon\B(\LL^{2}(\mathbb{G}))\ni x\mapsto(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\in\B(\LL^{2}(\mathbb{G})).

A similar formula holds for an arbitrary element of the Fourier-Stieltjes algebra, see the discussion after Proposition 4.1 in
[
18
]
.

Proof.

As before, the left centraliser associated with
a
is simply left multiplication by
\omega
, and so
\Theta^{l}(a)
has the given form. Then for
x\in\B(\LL^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
, using that
(\widehat{\Delta}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})=\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{23}
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\otimes\Phi(a)(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigl(((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigl((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}_{23}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}\mathrm{W}_{13}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{23})\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}_{13}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}_{13}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\otimes(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes x)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}),

and so
\Phi(a)
has indeed the claimed form.
∎

4.
The approximation property

We define the approximation property for a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
(abbreviated AP) in a way completely analogous to the definition of AP for locally compact groups by Haagerup and Kraus in
[
32
]
. Recall that we fix the predual
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, and we always refer to the corresponding weak
∗
-topology on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
has the
approximation property (AP)
if there is a net
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A(\mathbb{G})
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
-topology of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

Remark 4.2
.

•

We could call the above property “left AP” and introduce also a right variant of AP. However, in Proposition
4.14
we will show that these properties are equivalent, so that
there is no need to distinguish between them.

•

A variant of AP was considered by Kraus-Ruan
[
42
]
(for Kac algebras) and Crann in
[
14
]
. Their property is a priori stronger, but in Theorem
4.4
we show that this variant is in fact equivalent to Definition
4.1
. This proves a conjecture by Kraus-Ruan
[
42
, Remark 4.2]
.

Let us list some examples and counter-examples:

•

In Section
5
we show weak amenability implies AP, therefore all compact quantum groups and the discrete quantum groups
\widehat{O_{F}^{+}},\widehat{U_{F}^{+}}
have AP (
[
26
,
22
]
). Furthermore, the locally compact quantum group
\SU_{q}(1,1)_{ext}
has AP, see
[
11
]
.

•

Permanence properties of AP with respect to quantum subgroups (Theorem
7.1
), direct products (Proposition
7.22
), free products (Theorem
7.8
) and the
Drinfeld double construction (Theorem
7.16
) allow us to construct examples with and without AP. For instance, the Drinfeld double of
\operatorname{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
, or of any classical
