locally compact group without AP, see
[
48
]
, gives rise to non-classical quantum groups without AP. Similarly,
\operatorname{SL}(3,\mathbb{Z})\star\widehat{\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)}
is a quantum group without AP, as AP passes to quantum subgroups.

•

Examples of quantum groups with AP similarly arise. As AP passes to free products,

\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\star\widehat{\operatorname{SU}_{q}(2)}
is a quantum group with AP. Similarly,
D(\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}))^{\wedge}
, the dual of the quantum double of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, has AP (Theorem
7.16
).

•

The paper
[
24
]
by Fima, Mukherjee and Patri makes a careful study of compact bicrossed products, and in particular,
[
24
, Theorem 6.7]
provides estimates on the Cowling–Haagerup constant (see Definition
5.1
) of the resulting discrete quantum groups. Using this, if one starts with a discrete group
\Gamma
with AP and with
\Lambda_{cb}(\Gamma)\geq n
(see for example
[
10
, Theorem 12.3.8]
and references therein) then one can construct a non-classical discrete quantum group with AP but with Cowling–Haagerup constant
\geq n
. Then by taking infinite products we obtain a discrete quantum group which has AP but which is not weakly amenable.

•

We leave as a question whether the Drinfeld double of
\SU_{q}(3)
might be an example of a locally compact quantum group which does not have AP; for more see Remark
7.15
.

4.1.
Equivalent characterisations

We check first that the approximation property is preserved under taking the
commutant
quantum group
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
, or the
opposite
quantum group
\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}
, for definitions see
[
47
, Section 4]
.

Proposition 4.3
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbb{G}
has AP,

(2)

\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
has AP.

(3)

\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}
has AP,

Proof.

Assume that
\mathbb{G}
has AP, i.e. there is a net
(\omega_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
such that
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\omega_{i})\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
weak
∗
in
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

First we will prove that
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
has AP. Recall that
\widehat{\mathbb{G}^{\prime}}=\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\operatorname{op}}
(
[
47
, Proposition 4.2]
). Using Proposition
3.9
, choose an arbitrary functional
\Omega_{y,\nu}\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}^{\prime}))
where
\mathsf{H}
is a separable Hilbert space,
y\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
and
\nu\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
. Pick some selfadjoint antiunitary
\mathcal{J}
on
\mathsf{H}
(for example, pick an orthonormal basis and let
\mathcal{J}
be coordinate-wise complex conjugation) and define
j:\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
by
j(x)=\mathcal{J}x^{*}\mathcal{J}
, an
\star
-antihomomorphism with
j^{2}=\textnormal{id}
. Then
\widehat{R}\otimes j
is a well-defined bounded map on the spatial tensor product
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
, and
\widehat{R}_{*}\otimes j^{*}
is well-defined on
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
. Indeed,
\widehat{R}\otimes j
acts via
(\widehat{R}\otimes j)(X)=(J_{\varphi}\otimes\mathcal{J})X^{*}(J_{\varphi}\otimes\mathcal{J})
for
X\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
and then we can define
\widehat{R}_{*}\otimes j^{*}
as the restriction of
(\widehat{R}\otimes j)^{*}
to
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
:
(\widehat{R}\otimes j)^{*}
preserves this subspace as
(\widehat{R}\otimes j)^{*}(\omega_{\xi\otimes\eta})=\omega_{J_{\varphi}\xi\otimes\mathcal{J}\eta}
for
\xi\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}),\eta\in\mathsf{H}
. Since
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})^{*}\subseteq(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H}))^{*}
is closed, the claim follows. Furthermore, both these maps are isometric bijections.

Set
x=(\widehat{R}\otimes j)(y),\omega=(\widehat{R}_{*}\otimes j^{*})(\nu)
. Then using Lemma
3.10

\displaystyle\langle\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\omega_{i}),\Omega_{x,\omega}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle((\omega_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\widehat{R}\otimes j)(y),(\widehat{R}_{*}\otimes j^{*})(\nu)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\widehat{R}(\omega_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\widehat{R}\otimes\textnormal{id})(y),\nu\rangle=\langle((\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{R}_{*}(\omega_{i}))\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(y),\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle((\widehat{R}_{*}(\omega_{i})\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\operatorname{op}}}\otimes\textnormal{id})(y),\nu\rangle=\langle\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\operatorname{op}}}(\widehat{R}_{*}(\omega_{i})),\Omega_{y,\nu}\rangle

and since
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\omega_{i})\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
weak
∗
, we conclude
\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\operatorname{op}}}(\widehat{R}_{*}(\omega_{i}))\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
weak
∗
.
This shows that
\mathbb{G}^{\prime}
has AP.

Next we prove that
\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}
has AP. By
[
47
, Proposition 4.2]
we have
\widehat{\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}}=\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}
. Write
R^{\sim}
for the extension of the unitary antipode on
\mathbb{G}
,
\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))\ni x\mapsto J_{\widehat{\varphi}}x^{*}J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\in\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
, so that
\tilde{\omega}_{i}=\omega_{i}\circ R^{\sim}\in\LL^{1}({\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}^{\prime})
. We claim that the net
(\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}}(\omega_{i}\circ R^{\sim}))_{i\in I}
converges weak
∗
to
\mathbbm{1}
in
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}))
. Take
z\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H}),\theta\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
. Recall that
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime})=J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})J_{\widehat{\varphi}}
and

\begin{split}\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}}\colon\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{\prime}\ni x\mapsto&(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes J_{\widehat{\varphi}})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}xJ_{\widehat{\varphi}})(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes J_{\widehat{\varphi}})\\
=&(R^{\sim}\otimes R^{\sim})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(R^{\sim}(x))\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{\prime}\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})^{\prime}.\end{split}

Using this, we obtain

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}}(\omega_{i}\circ R^{\sim}),\Omega_{z,\theta}\rangle=\langle(\Theta^{l}(\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}}(\omega_{i}\circ R^{\sim}))\otimes\textnormal{id})z,\theta\rangle=\langle((\omega_{i}\circ R^{\sim}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}^{\prime}}\otimes\textnormal{id})z,\theta\rangle\\
&=\langle(R^{\sim}\otimes j)\bigl((\omega_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\textnormal{id}\bigr)(R^{\sim}\otimes j)z,\theta\rangle=\langle\lambda_{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\omega_{i}),\Omega_{(R^{\sim}\otimes j)z,\theta\circ(R^{\sim}\otimes j)}\rangle\\
&\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\langle\mathbbm{1},\Omega_{(R^{\sim}\otimes j)z,\theta\circ(R^{\sim}\otimes j)}\rangle=\langle z,\theta\rangle=\langle\mathbbm{1},\Omega_{z,\theta}\rangle\end{split}

and thus
\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}}
has AP. The converse implications follow since
(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})^{\prime}=\mathbb{G}
and
(\mathbb{G}^{\operatorname{op}})^{\operatorname{op}}=\mathbb{G}
.
∎

The next result shows that the version of the approximation property considered in
[
42
]
and
[
14
]
is equivalent to AP as defined in Definition
4.1
. Both
[
42
, Definition 4.1]
and
[
14
, Page 1728]
take condition (
2
) of the following theorem as their definition of AP.

Theorem 4.4
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbb{G}
has AP,

(2)

there is a net
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in the Fourier algebra
\A(\mathbb{G})
, such that the corresponding net
(\Theta^{l}(a_{i}))_{i\in I}
converges to the identity in the stable point-weak
∗
-topology of
\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.

In order to prove Theorem
4.4
we need to establish some preliminary results. Recall that
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
is a right
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
-module via
x\star\omega=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(x)

for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
a\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
.

(1)

If
a\in\A(\mathbb{G})
then
a\star\omega\in\A(\mathbb{G})
.

(2)

If
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
then
a\star\omega\in\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
\|a\star\omega\|_{cb}\leq\|a\|_{cb}\|\omega\|
and

\Theta^{l}(a\star\omega)(\widehat{x})=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x}){\mathrm{W}}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\big)\qquad(\widehat{x}\in L^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

Proof.

(1) Write
a=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})
for
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Then

\begin{split}&\quad\;a\star\omega=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta\bigl((\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id}){\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{13}{\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}_{12}\bigr)\\
&=(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl({\mathrm{W}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}((\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\bigr)=\widehat{\lambda}\bigl(\widehat{\omega}\bigl(\cdot\;(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\bigr)\bigr)\in\A(\mathbb{G})\end{split}

as required.

(2) As
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, there is a well-defined linear map
T
on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
given by

T(\widehat{x})=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\big)\qquad(\widehat{x}\in L^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

Clearly
T
is completely bounded with
\|T\|_{cb}\leq\|a\|_{cb}\|\omega\|
and weak
∗
-continuous. We first show that
T
is the adjoint of a centraliser,
equivalently, that
\widehat{\Delta}T=(T\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
. If
\widehat{x}\in L^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
then using
\widehat{\Delta}\Theta^{l}(a)=(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}
gives

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}T(\widehat{x})

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\Delta}\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{12}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{\Delta}(\widehat{x}))\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{12}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13}\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{12}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{\Delta}(\widehat{x}))\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{12}\bigr)\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{12}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(T\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}(\widehat{x}).

Consequently
T
is the adjoint of a centraliser, and so there exists
b\in\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
T=\Theta^{l}(b)
. Then
b\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega})=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}\circ T)
for each
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, equivalently,
(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(T\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})
. In other words, we have

(b\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes T)(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{23})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}\big),

or equivalently

\displaystyle b\otimes\mathbbm{1}

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{23})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\bigr)

(4.1)

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{23}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}\bigr).

Now,
(\mathbbm{1}\otimes a)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})
so
a\otimes\mathbbm{1}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
and hence

\displaystyle a\star\omega\otimes\mathbbm{1}

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(a)\otimes\mathbbm{1}=((\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\bigr)

(4.2)

\displaystyle=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{23}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}_{13})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{13}\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}_{23}\bigr).

As (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) agree, we conclude that
b=a\star\omega
. Thus
a\star\omega\in\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
\Theta^{l}(a\star\omega)=T
as required.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

For any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
and
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}),a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
we have
\omega\star(xa)\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
.

Proof.

As
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
is a closed
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
-submodule of
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
, by
[
61
, Lemma 2.1]
we know that
\omega=b\omega_{1}
for some
b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
\omega_{1}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
. Then

\omega\star(xa)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta(xa)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{1})\big(\Delta(x)\Delta(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\big).

As
a,b\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
we know that
\Delta(a)(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
, the minimal
C^{*}
-algebraic tensor product. By continuity, it hence suffices to prove that

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{1})\big(\Delta(x)(c\otimes d)\big)\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})

for
c,d\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
. However, this equals
\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes d\omega_{1})\Delta(x)\bigr)c
and by
[
61
, Theorem 2.4]
we know that
(\textnormal{id}\otimes d\omega_{1})\Delta(x)\in\M(\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G}))
, and so the result follows.
∎

Next we introduce certain functionals in
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
in analogy to
[
32
, Proposition 1.3]
. For a Hilbert space
\mathsf{H}
,
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
and
f\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
define

(4.3)

\Omega_{x,\omega,f}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\ni a\mapsto\langle(\Theta^{l}(a\star f)\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle\in\mathbb{C}.

Note that
\Omega_{x,\omega,f}
is well-defined and bounded by Proposition
4.5
.

Proposition 4.7
.

The linear functional
\Omega_{x,\omega,f}
is weak
∗
-continuous, hence is contained in
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.

Proof.

Clearly
\Omega_{x,\omega,f}
is bounded with
\|\Omega_{x,\omega,f}\|\leq\|x\|\|f\|\|\omega\|
, so it suffices to prove the result when
\omega
is in the algebraic tensor
product of
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
with
\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
, and hence by linearity, we may suppose that
\omega=\widehat{\omega}\otimes u
. Then

\Omega_{x,\omega,f}(a)=\langle(\Theta^{l}(a\star f)\otimes\textnormal{id})(x),\widehat{\omega}\otimes u\rangle=\langle\Theta^{l}(a\star f)((\textnormal{id}\otimes u)(x)),\widehat{\omega}\rangle\qquad(a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))).

Thus, it suffices to show that for
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\widehat{x}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})

\mu\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\ni a\mapsto\langle\Theta^{l}(a\star f)(\widehat{x}),\widehat{\omega}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}

is weak
∗
-continuous. By Proposition
4.5
, given the form of
\Theta^{l}(a\star f)
,

\mu(a)=\langle(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}},f\otimes\widehat{\omega}\rangle\qquad(a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))).

As
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, also
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}(f\otimes\widehat{\omega})\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\widehat{\otimes}\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, so again by approximation, it suffices to show that for
f^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G}),\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
the map

\mu^{\prime}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))\ni a\mapsto\langle(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a))((\mathbbm{1}\otimes\widehat{x})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}),f^{\prime}\otimes\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\Theta^{l}(a)(\widehat{x}\widehat{y}\big),\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}\rangle\in\mathbb{C}

is weak
∗
-continuous, where
\widehat{y}=(f^{\prime}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
.

By linear density of products, compare Lemma
5.3
, it suffices to consider the case when
\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}=\widehat{\omega}_{1}\star\widehat{\omega}_{2}
for
\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{2}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. As
\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\widehat{\omega}_{1}\star\widehat{\omega}_{2})=\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\widehat{\omega}_{1})\star\widehat{\omega}_{2}
by the left centraliser property, we see that

\mu^{\prime}(a)=\langle\Theta^{l}(a)(\widehat{x}\widehat{y}),\widehat{\omega}_{1}\star\widehat{\omega}_{2}\rangle=\langle\widehat{\omega}_{2}\star(\widehat{x}\widehat{y}),\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\widehat{\omega}_{1})\rangle\qquad(a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))).

As
\widehat{y}\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, by Lemma
4.6
applied to
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
, we know that
\widehat{\omega}_{2}\star(\widehat{x}\widehat{y})\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Thus
\mu^{\prime}\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
by Proposition
3.9
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
4.4
.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
follows directly from the characterisation of functionals in the predual
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
in Proposition
3.9
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Assume that
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
is a net in
\A(\mathbb{G})
which converges weak
∗
to
\mathbbm{1}
in
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Pick a state
f\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, and for each
i\in I
set
b_{i}=a_{i}\star f
. By Proposition
4.5
we have
b_{i}\in\A(\mathbb{G})
. We now show that
(\Theta^{l}(b_{i}))_{i\in I}
converges to the identity in the stable point-weak
∗
-topology.

Given a separable Hilbert space
\mathsf{H}
,
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\bar{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})
, and
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
, using Proposition
4.7
we see that

\begin{split}&\langle(\Theta^{l}(b_{i})\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle=\langle(\Theta^{l}(a_{i}\star f)\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle=\langle a_{i},\Omega_{x,\omega,f}\rangle\\
\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}&\langle\mathbbm{1},\Omega_{x,\omega,f}\rangle=\langle(\Theta^{l}(\mathbbm{1}\star f)\otimes\textnormal{id})(x),\omega\rangle,\end{split}

Since
f
is a state, we have
\mathbbm{1}\star f=(f\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(\mathbbm{1})=\mathbbm{1}
, hence
\Theta^{l}(\mathbbm{1}\star f)=\textnormal{id}
, and so
(\Theta^{l}(b_{i})\otimes\textnormal{id})(x)\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}x
weak
∗
as required.
∎

4.2.
Further general properties

Let us start with an auxiliary technical result. Recall that for a von Neumann algebra
\M
and a linear map
T\colon\M\rightarrow\M
we define
T^{\dagger}\colon\M\ni x\mapsto T(x^{*})^{*}\in\M
.

Lemma 4.8
.

If
T\in\CB^{\sigma}(\M)
, then
T^{\dagger}\in\CB^{\sigma}(\M)
and
\|T^{\dagger}\|_{cb}=\|T\|_{cb}
. If
\M=\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
for a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
then
R\circ T\circ R\in\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}))
and
\|R\circ T\circ R\|_{cb}=\|T\|_{cb}
. Both operations
T\mapsto T^{\dagger},T\mapsto R\circ T\circ R
are continuous with respect to the stable point-weak
∗
-topology.

Proof.

Let
\M\subseteq\B(\mathsf{H})
. Using the normal version of Wittstock’s Theorem (compare with the start of the proof of
[
33
, Theorem 2.5]
for example), we can find a Hilbert space
\mathsf{K}
, bounded linear maps
V,W\colon\mathsf{H}\rightarrow\mathsf{K}
and a normal representation
\pi\colon\M\rightarrow\B(\mathsf{K})
such that
T=W^{*}\pi(\cdot)V
and
\|T\|_{cb}=\|V\|\|W\|
. Now proving the claimed properties of
T^{\dagger}
is elementary.

Assume next that
\M=\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. If
T\in\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}))
, then clearly
R\circ T\circ R
is normal since the unitary antipode
R
is normal. Using again Wittstock’s Theorem,
write
T^{\dagger}=W^{*}\pi(\cdot)V
and choose an antiunitary
\mathcal{J}
on
\mathsf{H}
which satisfies
\mathcal{J}^{*}=\mathcal{J}
. Then, for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
,

\begin{split}&\quad\;R\circ T\circ R(x)=J_{\widehat{\varphi}}T(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}x^{*}J_{\widehat{\varphi}})^{*}J_{\widehat{\varphi}}=J_{\widehat{\varphi}}T^{\dagger}(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}xJ_{\widehat{\varphi}})J_{\widehat{\varphi}}\\
&=J_{\widehat{\varphi}}W^{*}\pi(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}xJ_{\widehat{\varphi}})VJ_{\widehat{\varphi}}=(\mathcal{J}WJ_{\widehat{\varphi}})^{*}\mathcal{J}\pi(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}xJ_{\widehat{\varphi}})\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{J}VJ_{\widehat{\varphi}}).\end{split}

As
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\ni x\mapsto\mathcal{J}\pi(J_{\widehat{\varphi}}xJ_{\widehat{\varphi}})\mathcal{J}\in\B(\mathsf{H})
is a
\star
-homomorphism, it follows that
R\circ T\circ R
is CB with
\|R\circ T\circ R\|_{cb}\leq\|T\|_{cb}
. Again, as
R\circ(R\circ T\circ R)\circ R=T
, we have in fact
\|R\circ T\circ R\|_{cb}=\|T\|_{cb}
.

Let
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
be a net in
\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}))
converging to
T
in the stable point-weak
∗
-topology. Choose a self-adjoint antiunitary
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
on
\ell^{2}
and define
j=\mathcal{J}^{\prime}(\cdot)^{*}\mathcal{J}^{\prime},
a normal
\star
-antiautomorphism of
\B(\ell^{2})
. Then
R\otimes j
extends to a well-defined normal bounded linear map on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\B(\ell^{2})
. Indeed, in the proof of Proposition
4.3
we argued that
R_{*}\otimes(j|_{\mathcal{K}(\ell^{2})})^{*}
is a bounded linear map on
\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\ell^{2})_{*}
, and we just need to take the dual map
R\otimes j=(R_{*}\otimes(j|_{\mathcal{K}(\ell^{2})})^{*})^{*}
. For
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}\B(\ell^{2}),\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\mathbb{G})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\ell^{2})_{*}
we have

\begin{split}&\langle(R\circ T_{i}\circ R\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle=\langle(R\circ T_{i}\circ R\otimes j^{2})x,\omega\rangle=\langle(T_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((R\otimes j)(x)\bigr),\omega\circ(R\otimes j)\rangle\\
\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}&\langle(T\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((R\otimes j)(x)\bigr),\omega\circ(R\otimes j)\rangle=\langle(R\circ T\circ R\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle,\end{split}

which concludes the proof.
∎

We now show that
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
admits an interesting involution. Recall that
S
denotes the antipode of
\mathbb{G}
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Then
a^{*}\in\Dom(S)
and
S(a^{*})\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
\Theta^{l}(S(a^{*}))=\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.8
, we know that
\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}\in\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}))
. One easily checks that
\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}
is a left
\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
-module map, hence
\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}=\Theta^{l}(b)
for some
b\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. The claim follows now from
[
17
, Theorem 5.9]
.

For the convenience of the reader let us also indicate a direct argument. As
\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}=\Theta^{l}(b)
we have that

(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(\Theta^{l}(b)\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(\Theta^{l}(a)^{\dagger}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=((\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*})^{*},

and hence

\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b^{*})=(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}*}\quad\Rightarrow\quad(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})b^{*}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}\circ\Theta^{l}(a))\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}

for each
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. In what follows, we treat
S
as a densely-defined, closed operator on
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
equipped with the weak
∗
-topology. From
[
70
, Proposition 2.24]
, compare
[
46
, Proposition 8.3]
, we know that for any
\widehat{\omega}
we get
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\in D(S)
and
S((\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}
. It follows that
(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})b^{*}\in D(S)
with

\displaystyle S\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})b^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}\circ\Theta^{l}(a))\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}=(\widehat{\omega}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=a\,(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})

(4.4)

\displaystyle=a\,(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}=a\,S\big((\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\big).

Let
C=\{(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}\,|\,\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\}\subseteq D(S)
. We shall show that
C
contains a net
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
such that both
a_{i}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
and
S(a_{i})\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
weak
∗
in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. It follows that
a_{i}b^{*}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}b^{*}
and
aS(a_{i})\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}a
weak
∗
, and so as
S
is weak
∗
-closed, it follows from (
4.4
) that
b^{*}\in D(S)
with
S(b^{*})=a
. Hence
a^{*}=S(b^{*})^{*}\in\Dom(S)
and
S(a^{*})=S(S(b^{*})^{*})=b
, as claimed.

We now show the claim about
C
, using some standard “smearing” techniques, compare
[
43
]
. For
a\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
and
r>0,z\in\mathbb{C}
, define

a(r,z)=\frac{r}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp(-r^{2}(t-z)^{2})\tau_{t}(a)\operatorname{d}\!t.

Then
a(r,z)
is analytic for the one-parameter automorphism group
(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
with
\tau_{w}(a(r,z))=a(r,z+w)
for
w\in\mathbb{C}
. Similarly, for
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, define

\widehat{\omega}(r,z)=\frac{r}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp(-r^{2}(t-z)^{2})\widehat{\omega}\circ\widehat{\tau}_{t}\operatorname{d}\!t.

Given
\widehat{\omega}
, let
a=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})
. As
(\tau_{t}\otimes\widehat{\tau}_{t})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})=\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
, it follows that
\tau_{t}(a)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}\circ\widehat{\tau}_{-t})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})
and hence
a(r,z)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}(r,-z))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})
. Finally, as
S=R\tau_{-i/2}
, it follows that
S(a(r,z))=R(a(r,z-i/2))=(\textnormal{id}\otimes(\widehat{\omega}\circ\widehat{R})(r,-z+i/2))(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})
.

As
C
is norm dense in
\mathrm{C}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
, we can find a net
(\widehat{\omega}_{i})_{i\in I}
with
a_{i}=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}_{i})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
strictly. By
[
43
, Proposition 2.25]
, the net
(a_{i}(r,z))_{i\in I}
converges strictly to
\mathbbm{1}(r,z)=\mathbbm{1}
, for any choice of
r,z
. By the above discussion,
a_{i}(r,z)\in C
. Then also
S(a_{i}(r,z))=R(a_{i}(r,z-i/2))\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}R(\mathbbm{1})=\mathbbm{1}
strictly. Cohen–Hewitt’s Factorisation Theorem shows that strict convergence implies weak
∗
-convergence in
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, hence we have constructed the required net.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Then
\Theta^{l}(a)\in\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
preserves the adjoint if and only if
S(a^{*})=a
.

Next we check that
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is globally invariant under the scaling and modular automorphism groups.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group and let
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

•

\tau_{t}(a)\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with
\Theta^{l}(\tau_{t}(a))=\hat{\tau}_{t}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t}
.

•

\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a)\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with

\Theta^{l}(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a))\colon x\mapsto\hat{\delta}^{it}\,\hat{\tau}_{t}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\bigl(\hat{\delta}^{-it}\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x)\bigr)
.

•

\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(a)\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
with

\Theta^{l}(\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(a))\colon x\mapsto\hat{\tau}_{-t}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\bigl(\hat{\tau}_{t}(x)\hat{\delta}^{-it}\bigr)\hat{\delta}^{it}
.

Proof.

Take
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Using
(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\tau_{t})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
(see proof of
[
46
, Proposition 6.10]
) we obtain

\begin{split}&\quad\;\tau_{t}(a)\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\tau_{t}\bigl(a\,\tau_{-t}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega))\bigr)=\tau_{t}\bigl(a\widehat{\lambda}(\omega\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})\bigr)=\widehat{\lambda}\bigl(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\omega\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t}\bigr)\end{split}

which implies
\tau_{t}(a)\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
and
\Theta^{l}(\tau_{t}(a))=\hat{\tau}_{t}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t}
; notice that clearly the right-hand-side of this final expression gives a completely bounded map.

We now use the following facts. Firstly, by the definition of
\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}
we have
(\rho\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\rho\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(x))
for
\rho\in\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G}),x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
. Secondly,
(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\otimes\sigma^{\psi}_{-t})\circ\Delta=\Delta\circ\tau_{t}
, see
[
46
, Proposition 6.8]
. Thus

\begin{split}&\quad\;(\rho\circ\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\rho\circ\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(x))=\Lambda_{\varphi}\bigl((\rho\otimes\sigma^{\psi}_{t})\Delta(\tau_{t}(x))\bigr)\\
&=\nu^{\frac{t}{2}}\nabla_{\psi}^{it}\Lambda_{\varphi}((\rho\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(\tau_{t}(x)))=\nabla_{\psi}^{it}(\rho\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})P^{it}\Lambda_{\varphi}(x),\end{split}

which implies that
(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})=(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\nabla_{\psi}^{it})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes P^{it})
; here we have also used
[
70
, Definition 5.1, Remark 5.2]
. Next, since
\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=\chi(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})
and
\hat{\delta}^{-it}=\nabla_{\psi}^{it}P^{it}
(
[
70
, Theorem 5.17]
), and using that
\hat{\tau}_{-t}(y)=P^{-it}yP^{it}
for
y\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
see
[
46
, Proposition 8.23]
, we arrive at

(4.5)

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\sigma^{\varphi}_{t})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})=(\nabla_{\psi}^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(P^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})=(\hat{\delta}^{-it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})(\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}).

Using this formula and
\hat{\tau}_{t}(\hat{\delta})=\hat{\delta}
(
[
70
, Theorem 3.11]
) we calculate

\begin{split}&\quad\;\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a)\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\bigl(a\sigma^{\varphi}_{-t}(\widehat{\lambda}(\omega))\bigr)=\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\bigl(a(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\hat{\delta}^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})\bigr)\bigr)\\
&=\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\bigl(a\widehat{\lambda}((\omega\hat{\delta}^{it})\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})\bigr)=\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}\bigl(\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}((\omega\hat{\delta}^{it})\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}))\bigr)\\
&=\widehat{\lambda}\bigl(\bigl(\bigl(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}((\omega\hat{\delta}^{it})\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})\bigr)\hat{\delta}^{-it}\bigr)\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t}\bigr).\end{split}

The above shows that
\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a)\in\M^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, and for
x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
we get

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle\Theta^{l}(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a))(x),\omega\rangle=\langle x,\Theta^{l}(\sigma^{\varphi}_{t}(a))_{*}(\omega)\rangle=\langle x,\bigl(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}((\omega\hat{\delta}^{it})\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})\hat{\delta}^{-it}\bigr)\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t}\rangle\\
&=\langle\hat{\delta}^{-it}\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x),\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}((\omega\hat{\delta}^{it})\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})\rangle=\langle\hat{\delta}^{it}\hat{\tau}_{t}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\bigl(\hat{\delta}^{-it}\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x)\bigr),\omega\rangle.\end{split}

The final claim is shown in a similar way. We have
(\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\otimes\tau_{-t})\circ\Delta=\Delta\circ\sigma^{\psi}_{t}
(
[
46
, Proposition 8.23]
) and hence for
x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
,

\begin{split}&\quad\;(\rho\circ\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\rho\circ\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(x))=\Lambda_{\varphi}\bigl((\rho\otimes\tau_{t})\Delta(\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(x))\bigr)\\
&=\nu^{-\frac{t}{2}}P^{it}\Lambda_{\varphi}((\rho\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(x)))=P^{it}(\rho\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\nabla_{\psi}^{it}\Lambda_{\varphi}(x),\end{split}

consequently
(\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})=(\mathbbm{1}\otimes P^{it})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\nabla_{\psi}^{it})
and so

(\textnormal{id}\otimes\sigma^{\psi}_{t})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})=(P^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}(\nabla_{\psi}^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})=(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})(\hat{\delta}^{-it}\otimes\mathbbm{1}).

Calculating as before,

\begin{split}&\quad\;\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(a)\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\bigl(a(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}})(\hat{\delta}^{it}\otimes\mathbbm{1})\bigr)\bigr)\\
&=\sigma^{\psi}_{t}\bigl(a\widehat{\lambda}((\hat{\delta}^{it}\omega)\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t})\bigr)=\widehat{\lambda}\bigl(\bigl(\bigl(\hat{\delta}^{-it}\,\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}((\hat{\delta}^{it}\omega)\circ\hat{\tau}_{-t})\bigr)\bigr)\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}\bigr)\end{split}

and so

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle\Theta^{l}(\sigma^{\psi}_{t}(a))(x),\omega\rangle=\langle\hat{\tau}_{-t}\circ\Theta^{l}(a)\bigl(\hat{\tau}_{t}(x)\hat{\delta}^{-it}\bigr)\hat{\delta}^{it},\omega\rangle\qquad(x\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\end{split}

as desired.
∎

In the next proposition we show that for any
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
the map
\Theta^{l}(a)
is bounded on the Hilbert space level; the final claim should be compared with
Proposition
4.9
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Then for
b\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
we have
\Theta^{l}(a)(b)\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
, and the densely defined operator

\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})\supseteq\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}})\ni\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b)\mapsto\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\Theta^{l}(a)(b))\in\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})

is bounded. In fact, we have

\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\Theta^{l}(a)(b))=S^{-1}(a)\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b)=S(a^{*})^{*}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b)\qquad(b\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}).

Here and in the sequel we use the GNS implementation of map
\Theta^{l}(a)
– in the literature people have considered also different embeddings of (a subspace of)
\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
into
\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})
, c.f.
[
62
, Section 2]
.

In order to prove Proposition
4.12
we start with a general lemma; recall (
2.3
) for the definition of
\mathscr{J}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\omega\in\mathscr{J}\subseteq\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, let
a\in\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
, and let
b\in\Dom(\sigma^{\varphi}_{-i/2})\subseteq\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. Then
a\omega b\in\mathscr{J}
with
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(a\omega b))=aJ_{\varphi}\sigma^{\varphi}_{-i/2}(b)^{*}J_{\varphi}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega))
.

Proof.

Take
x\in\mathfrak{N}_{\varphi}
. We have

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle x^{*},a\omega b\rangle=\langle(a^{*}xb^{*})^{*},\omega\rangle=\langle\Lambda_{\varphi}(a^{*}xb^{*})\,|\,\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega))\rangle\\
&=\langle a^{*}J_{\varphi}\sigma^{\varphi}_{i/2}(b^{*})^{*}J_{\varphi}\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\,|\,\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega))\rangle=\langle\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\,|\,aJ_{\varphi}\sigma^{\varphi}_{i/2}(b^{*})J_{\varphi}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega))\rangle\end{split}

which proves the claim.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.12
.

Take
b=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}
for
\omega\in\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G})
such that
\overline{\omega}\in\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})
and
\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\in\mathscr{J}
; that such an
\omega
exists follows from Lemma
2.1
, for example. Then, for any
\widehat{\omega}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
,

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle\Theta^{l}(a)(b),\widehat{\omega}\rangle=\langle(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}},\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\widehat{\omega})\rangle=\langle\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\widehat{\omega})),\omega\rangle\\
&=\langle a\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}),\omega\rangle=\langle(\widehat{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}},\omega a\rangle=\langle(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega a)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}},\widehat{\omega}\rangle,\end{split}

which shows that

\Theta^{l}(a)(b)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega a)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}.

Observe also that

(4.6)

b=(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})=((\overline{\omega}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}})^{*}=(\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=\lambda(\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}),

in particular
b\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
. Now, by Proposition
4.9
we have
a^{*}\in D(S)
, hence for
y\in\Dom(S)
it holds that
S(y)^{*}a^{*}\in\Dom(S)
(as
S(y)^{*}\in\Dom(S)
and
\Dom(S)
is closed under multiplication
[
46
, Proposition 5.22]
), consequently

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle S(y),\overline{\overline{\omega a}}\rangle=\langle aS(y),\omega\rangle=\overline{\langle S(y)^{*}a^{*},\overline{\omega}\rangle}=\overline{\langle S(y)^{*}a^{*},\overline{\omega}^{\sharp\sharp}\rangle}\\
&=\overline{\langle S(S(y)^{*}a^{*}),\overline{\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}}\rangle}=\langle yS(a^{*})^{*},\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\rangle=\langle yS^{-1}(a),\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\rangle=\langle y,S^{-1}(a)\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\rangle\end{split}

hence
\overline{\omega a}\in\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G})
and
\overline{\omega a}^{\sharp}=S^{-1}(a)\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}
. Consequently

\Theta^{l}(a)(b)=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega a)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}=(\overline{\omega a}^{\sharp}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=(S^{-1}(a)\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\otimes\textnormal{id})\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}}=\lambda(S^{-1}(a)\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}).

This calculation, combined with Lemma
4.13
, shows that
\Theta^{l}(a)(b)\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
with

\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\Theta^{l}(a)(b))=\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(S^{-1}(a)\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}))=S^{-1}(a)\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}))=S^{-1}(a)\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b).

Now let
b\in\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
be arbitrary. By Lemma
2.1
the space

\begin{split}&\quad\;\{(\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega)\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbb{G}}}\,|\,\omega\in\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G})\colon\overline{\omega}\in\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G}),\,\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\in\mathscr{J}\}\\
&=\{\lambda(\overline{\omega}^{\sharp})\,|\,\omega\in\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G})\colon\overline{\omega}\in\LL^{1}_{\sharp}(\mathbb{G}),\,\overline{\omega}^{\sharp}\in\mathscr{J}\}\end{split}

is a
\sigma\textsc{-sot}\times\|\cdot\|
core for
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}
. Hence there is a net
(\omega_{i})_{i\in I}
of suitable functionals with
b=\sigma\textsc{-sot}-\lim_{i\in I}\lambda(\omega_{i})
and
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b)=\lim_{i\in I}\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega_{i}))
. By
\sigma
-
sot
-continuity of
\Theta^{l}(a)
and the previous reasoning we obtain

\Theta^{l}(a)(\lambda(\omega_{i}))\xrightarrow[i\in I]{\sigma\textsc{-sot}}\Theta^{l}(a)(b)

and

\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\Theta^{l}(a)(\lambda(\omega_{i})))=S^{-1}(a)\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega_{i}))\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}S^{-1}(a)\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b).

Thus, since
\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}
is
\sigma\textsc{-sot}\times\|\cdot\|
closed,

\Theta^{l}(a)(b)\in\Dom(\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}})=\mathfrak{N}_{\widehat{\varphi}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(\Theta^{l}(a)(b))=S^{-1}(a)\Lambda_{\widehat{\varphi}}(b)

as claimed.
∎

4.3.
Left versus right

We have defined AP of a locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
in Definition
4.1
using left CB multipliers. Let us verify that we would have obtained the same notion using
right CB multipliers.

Proposition 4.14
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbb{G}
has AP, i.e. there is a net in
\A(\mathbb{G})
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
-topology of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
,

(2)

there is a net in
\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
-topology of
\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
.

Proof.

Assume that
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
is a net in
\A(\mathbb{G})
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
-topology of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Let
\widehat{R}^{\sim}\colon\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))\rightarrow\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G}))
be the extension of the unitary antipode of
\mathbb{G}
given by
\widehat{R}^{\sim}(x)=J_{\varphi}x^{*}J_{\varphi}\,(x\in\B(\operatorname{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G})))
. For each
i\in I
there is
\omega_{i}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
with
a_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}(\omega_{i})
, and so by Lemma
3.1
, if we define
b^{\prime}_{i}=\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a_{i})
then
b^{\prime}_{i}=\widehat{\rho}(\omega_{i}\circ\widehat{R})\in\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\subseteq\M^{r}_{cb}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
with
\Theta^{r}(b^{\prime}_{i})=\widehat{R}\circ\Theta^{l}(a_{i})\circ\widehat{R}\in\CB^{\sigma}(\operatorname{L}^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
.

Let us now argue that
b^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{\w^{*}}\mathbbm{1}
: choose a functional
\theta\in Q^{r}(\widehat{\rho}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})))
. We have to show that
\theta\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim}\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
(this makes sense by Lemma
3.1
). We can write
\theta=\lim_{j\in J}\alpha^{r}(\nu_{j})
for some
\nu_{j}\in\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G}^{\prime})
(see Section
3.2
). Then, as
\widehat{R}^{\sim}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))\rightarrow\M^{r}_{cb}(\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}))
is an isometry (Lemma
4.8
) we obtain

\begin{split}&\quad\;\|\theta\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim}-\alpha^{l}(\nu_{j}\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim})\|=\sup_{a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))_{1}}|\langle\theta\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim}-\alpha^{l}(\nu_{j}\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim}),a\rangle|\\
&=\sup_{a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))_{1}}|\langle\theta-\alpha^{r}(\nu_{j}),\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a)\rangle|=\sup_{b^{\prime}\in\M^{r}_{cb}(\A(\mathbb{G}))_{1}}|\langle\theta-\alpha^{r}(\nu_{j}),b^{\prime}\rangle|=\|\theta-\alpha^{r}(\nu_{j})\|\xrightarrow[j\in J]{}0,\end{split}

so
\theta\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim}=\lim_{j\in J}\alpha^{l}(\nu_{j}\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim})\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
.
Now we can calculate

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle\mathbbm{1}-b^{\prime}_{i},\theta\rangle=\langle\mathbbm{1}-\widehat{R}^{\sim}(a_{i}),\theta\rangle=\langle\mathbbm{1}-a_{i},\theta\circ\widehat{R}^{\sim}\rangle\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}0,\end{split}

which shows that
(b^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a net giving us condition
(2)
. The converse implication is analogous.
∎

5.
Relation to other approximation properties

In this section we discuss the relation between AP and other approximation properties for locally compact quantum groups which have been studied in the literature.
Recall the following definitions, compare
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
,
[
9
, Section 5.2]
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group.

•

\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is
coamenable
if
\A(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded approximate identity,

•

\mathbb{G}
is
weakly amenable
if
\A(\mathbb{G})
has a left approximate identity
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
which is bounded in
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. In this case, the smallest number
C
for which we can choose net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
with
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq C\,(i\in I)
is the
Cowling–Haagerup constant
of
\mathbb{G}
, denoted
\Lambda_{cb}(\mathbb{G})
.

Remark 5.2
.

If
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a left approximate identity for
\A(\mathbb{G})
then
(R(e_{i}))_{i\in I}
is a right approximate identity, where
R
is the unitary antipode on
\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
. Indeed, let
\widehat{R}
be the unitary antipode on
\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. As
R\widehat{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{R}_{*}
, each
R(e_{i})
is a member of
\A(\mathbb{G})
, and as
\widehat{R}_{*}
is anti-multiplicative, it follows that
(R(e_{i}))_{i\in I}
is indeed a right approximate identity.
Thus it does not matter if we work in
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
or in
\M_{cb}^{r}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
, see also Lemma
4.8
.

We shall show that if
\mathbb{G}
has the AP, and the approximating net can be chosen in an appropriately bounded way, then
\mathbb{G}
will enjoy one of the stronger properties
in Definition
5.1
.

Let us first record some general results. For a proof of the following fact see for example
[
13
, Section 3]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
, the linear span of
\{ab\,|\,a,b\in\A(\mathbb{G})\}
is dense in
\A(\mathbb{G})
.

Next we recall a standard result in Banach algebra theory which follows from the Hahn–Banach Theorem and the fact that convex sets have the same norm and weak closures; see for
example
[
53
, Theorem 5.1.2(e)]
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
A
be a Banach algebra which has a weak bounded left approximate identity, meaning that there is a bounded net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
in
A
such that
\mu(e_{i}a-a)\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}0
for each
a\in A,\mu\in A^{*}
. Then
A
has a bounded left approximate identity (of the same bound).

Proposition
5.4
does not say that the weak blai (bounded left approximate identity) is itself a blai, but rather that having a weak blai implies there is a possibly different net forming a blai.

Let us denote by
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
the closure of
\A(\mathbb{G})
inside
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
and use Proposition
5.4

to obtain the following result, compare
[
25
, Proposition 1]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

The inclusion map
\A(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
is an injective contraction. The locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
is weakly amenable with Cowling–Haagerup constant at
most
K
if and only if
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded left approximate identity of bound at most
K
.

Proof.

Let
\omega\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
be an element of norm one. Then the map

\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\colon\nu\mapsto\omega\otimes\nu

is a complete isometry, and so applying
\widehat{\Delta}_{*}
shows that
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\rightarrow\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\colon\nu\mapsto\omega\star\nu
is a complete contraction. Thus
\|\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\|_{cb}\leq\|\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)\|
, here and below writing
\|\cdot\|_{cb}
for the norm on
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
and
\|\cdot\|
for the norm on
\A(\mathbb{G})
. The diagram (
3.1
) shows in particular that
\A(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
is injective, and hence
\A(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
is injective.

Now suppose that
\mathbb{G}
is weakly amenable and that
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a left approximate identity for
\A(\mathbb{G})
with
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq K
for each
i
. Then
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a bounded net in
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
. For
x\in\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
and
\epsilon>0
there is
a\in\A(\mathbb{G})
with
\|x-a\|_{cb}<\epsilon
, and there is
i_{0}
so that
\|e_{i}a-a\|<\epsilon
when
i\geq i_{0}
. Thus

\|e_{i}x-x\|_{cb}\leq\|e_{i}x-e_{i}a\|_{cb}+\|e_{i}a-a\|_{cb}+\|a-x\|_{cb}<K\epsilon+\epsilon+\epsilon\qquad(i\geq i_{0}).

It follows that
e_{i}x\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}x
in
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
. Consequently
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a blai for
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
of bound
\leq K
.

Conversely, suppose that
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded left approximate identity of bound
K
, say
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
. For
(i,n)\in I\times\mathbb{N}
pick
e_{i,n}\in\A(\mathbb{G})
with
\|e_{i,n}\|_{cb}=\|f_{i}\|_{cb}
and
\|e_{i,n}-f_{i}\|_{cb}<\tfrac{1}{n}
. For
x\in\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
and
\epsilon>0
there is
i_{0}
so that
\|f_{i}x-x\|_{cb}<\epsilon
for
i\geq i_{0}
. With
n>\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}
,

\|e_{i,n}x-x\|_{cb}\leq\|e_{i,n}x-f_{i}x\|_{cb}+\|f_{i}x-x\|_{cb}<\varepsilon\|x\|_{cb}+\varepsilon,

and so
e_{i,n}x\xrightarrow[(i,n)\in I\times\mathbb{N}]{}x
. We conclude that we may assume that
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
was actually a net in
\A(\mathbb{G})
and
\|f_{i}\|_{cb}\leq K
for each
i
. It remains to show that
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is a left approximate identity for
\A(\mathbb{G})
. Given
a\in\A(\mathbb{G})
and
\epsilon>0
, by Lemma
5.3
,
we can find elements
a_{k},b_{k}\in\A(\mathbb{G})
for
k=1,\dots,n
for some
n
such that
a_{0}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}b_{k}\in\A(\mathbb{G})
is within
\epsilon
distance of
a
. Then

\displaystyle\|f_{i}a-a\|

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{i}a-f_{i}a_{0}\|+\|f_{i}a_{0}-a_{0}\|+\|a_{0}-a\|

\displaystyle\leq K\|a-a_{0}\|+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|f_{i}a_{k}b_{k}-a_{k}b_{k}\|+\|a_{0}-a\|

\displaystyle\leq(K+1)\epsilon+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|f_{i}a_{k}-a_{k}\|_{cb}\|b_{k}\|.

Here we used that for
x\in\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
and
y\in\A(\mathbb{G})
we have
\|xy\|\leq\|x\|_{cb}\|y\|
. As
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is a left approximate identity in
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
, if
i
is sufficiently large then
\|f_{i}a_{k}-a_{k}\|_{cb}\|b_{k}\|<\tfrac{\epsilon}{n}
for each
k
, and so
\|f_{i}a-a\|\leq(K+2)\epsilon
. Hence
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is a left approximate identity for
\A(\mathbb{G})
.
∎

The following is an improvement on
[
32
, Theorem 1.13]
in the classical situation; Haagerup and Kraus only consider the case where
each element of the approximating net comes from a state.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbb{G}
has the approximation property, and we can choose the approximating net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A(\mathbb{G})
to be bounded;

(2)

\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is coamenable.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathbb{G}
has the approximation property with approximating net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
which is bounded in
\A(\mathbb{G})
. By definition,
e_{i}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
weak
∗
in
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. By Proposition
4.7
, we consider elements of the form
\Omega_{\widehat{x},\widehat{\omega},f}\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
. Here we will just consider
\widehat{x}\in\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\widehat{\omega}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, with
f\in\LL^{1}(\mathbb{G})
a state. According to equation (
4.3
), we get

\lim_{i\in I}\langle\Theta^{l}(e_{i}\star f)(\widehat{x}),\widehat{\omega}\rangle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle e_{i},\Omega_{\widehat{x},\widehat{\omega},f}\rangle=\langle\mathbbm{1},\Omega_{\widehat{x},\widehat{\omega},f}\rangle=\langle\Theta^{l}(\mathbbm{1}\star f)(\widehat{x}),\widehat{\omega}\rangle=\langle\widehat{x},\widehat{\omega}\rangle,

using
\mathbbm{1}\star f=\mathbbm{1}
and
\Theta^{l}(\mathbbm{1})=\textnormal{id}
in the last step.
For each
i\in I
let
e_{i}
be associated to
\widehat{\omega}_{i}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, so that
e_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}_{i})
. Then

\displaystyle e_{i}\star f

\displaystyle=(f\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta(e_{i})=(f\otimes\textnormal{id})\Delta((\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}_{i})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}))=(f\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}_{i})(\mathrm{W}_{23}^{\mathbb{G}*}\mathrm{W}_{13}^{\mathbb{G}*})

\displaystyle=(\textnormal{id}\otimes\widehat{\omega}_{i})\bigl(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes(f\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*}))\bigr)=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}),

where
\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}=(f\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\widehat{\omega}_{i}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Notice that
\|\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}\|\leq\|f\|\|\widehat{\omega}_{i}\|=\|\widehat{\omega}_{i}\|
.

As computed in Lemma
3.10
, it follows that
\Theta^{l}(e_{i}\star f)(\widehat{x})=(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}(\widehat{x})
, so we see that

\lim_{i\in I}\langle\widehat{x},\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}\star\widehat{\omega}\rangle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}\otimes\textnormal{id})\widehat{\Delta}(\widehat{x}),\widehat{\omega}\rangle=\langle\widehat{x},\widehat{\omega}\rangle\qquad(\widehat{x}\in\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}),\widehat{\omega}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})).

Thus
(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a weak bounded left approximate identity for
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
.
By Proposition
5.4
, we obtain that
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
has a bounded left approximate identity.
This already implies that
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is coamenable, see
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Conversely, suppose that
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
is coamenable, so that in particular,
\A(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded left approximate identity
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
. For each
i
, let
\widehat{\omega}_{i}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
with
\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}_{i})=e_{i}
, so
(\widehat{\omega}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a bounded net, and
\widehat{\omega}_{i}\star\widehat{\omega}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\widehat{\omega}
in norm, for each
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Thus, given
x\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
and
\widehat{\omega}\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
, we find that

\langle\Theta^{l}(e_{i})(x),\widehat{\omega}\rangle=\langle x,\widehat{\omega}_{i}\star\widehat{\omega}\rangle\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\langle x,\widehat{\omega}\rangle.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a separable Hilbert space.
As the net
(\Theta^{l}(e_{i}))_{i\in I}
is bounded, given
x\in\mathrm{C}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
and
\omega\in\operatorname{L}^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
, we find that

\lim_{i\in I}\Omega_{x,\omega}(e_{i})=\lim_{i\in I}\langle(\Theta^{l}(e_{i})\otimes\textnormal{id})x,\omega\rangle=\langle x,\omega\rangle=\Omega_{x,\omega}(\mathbbm{1}).

By Proposition
3.9
, it follows that
a_{i}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
weak
∗
in
\M_{cb}^{l}(\A(\mathbb{G}))
as required.
∎

The following is
[
32
, Theorem 1.12]
in the classical situation.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a locally compact quantum group. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathbb{G}
has the approximation property, and we can choose the approximating net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A(\mathbb{G})
to be bounded with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{cb}
;

(2)

\mathbb{G}
is weakly amenable.

Furthermore, in this case, the Cowling–Haagerup constant is at most the bound of
(e_{i})_{\in I}
, while when
\mathbb{G}
is weakly amenable, we can choose each
e_{i}
with
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq\Lambda_{cb}(\mathbb{G})
.

Proof.

We proceed as in the previous proof, starting with a net
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A(\mathbb{G})
, but now only with
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq K
for each
i
. We set
e_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}_{i})

and
\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i}=(f\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\mathbb{G}*})\widehat{\omega}_{i}
, where
f
is some fixed state and we are considering the natural (left)
\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
-module structure
on
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Then
(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
is a weak left approximate identity for
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}})
. Furthermore,
\|\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i})\|_{cb}=\|e_{i}\star f\|_{cb}\leq\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\|f\|\leq K
by Proposition
4.5
.

For each
i
let
f_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}(\widehat{\omega}^{\prime}_{i})\in\A(\mathbb{G})
. Let
\theta\colon\A(\mathbb{G})\rightarrow\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
be the inclusion map, and consider the
adjoint,
\theta^{*}\colon\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})^{*}\rightarrow\A(\mathbb{G})^{*}
. For
\mu\in\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})^{*}
and
a\in\A(\mathbb{G})
we see that

\lim_{i\in I}\langle\mu,\theta(f_{i})\theta(a)\rangle=\lim_{i\in I}\langle\theta^{*}(\mu),f_{i}a\rangle=\langle\theta^{*}(\mu),a\rangle=\langle\mu,\theta(a)\rangle.

As
\theta
has dense range, and
(\theta(f_{i}))_{i\in I}
is bounded in
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
, it follows that
(\theta(f_{i}))_{i\in I}
is a weak bounded left approximate identity. By Proposition
5.4
,
\A_{cb}^{l}(\mathbb{G})
has a bounded left approximate identity, and so Lemma
5.5
shows that
\mathbb{G}
is weakly amenable,
with Cowling–Haagerup constant at most
K
.

Conversely, when
\mathbb{G}
is weakly amenable, we can choose a left approximate identity
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
for
\A(\mathbb{G})
with
\|e_{i}\|_{cb}\leq\Lambda_{cb}(\mathbb{G})
for each
i
. The argument in the proof of Proposition
5.6
still works in this situation, because
(\Theta^{l}(e_{i}))_{i\in I}
is a net bounded with respect to the CB norm.
∎

6.
Discrete quantum groups and operator algebraic approximation properties

If the quantum group being studied is discrete, we can obtain better results. In Proposition
6.5
we will show that the net exhibiting AP can be chosen to have additional properties. We also relate AP to approximation properties of the associated operator algebras, in both the locally compact case, Proposition
6.10
, and the discrete case, Proposition
6.12
.

For the rest of this section
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
stands for an arbitary discrete quantum group. Then
\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}
is a compact quantum group, and we freely use the additional theory available in the compact case. We follow
[
51
]
as well as
[
49
,
66
]
, being aware that we use the “left” convention for multiplicative unitaries and representations.

Every irreducible unitary representation of
\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}
is finite-dimensional, and we denote by
\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold{\Gamma}})
the collection of equivalence classes of irreducibles.
We write
\overline{\alpha}
for the conjugate of
\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold{\Gamma}})
. For each
\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold{\Gamma}})
let
U^{\alpha}\in\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\otimes\B(\mathsf{H}_{\alpha})
be a unitary representation in the class of
\alpha
.
With respect to an orthonormal basis of
\mathsf{H}_{\alpha}
we regard
U^{\alpha}
as a matrix
[U^{\alpha}_{i,j}]_{1\leq i,j\leq\dim(\alpha)}
. The
matrix coefficients

U^{\alpha}_{i,j}
span a dense Hopf
\star
-algebra
\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\subseteq\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
. We denote by
h
the Haar integral on
\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
and
\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
, and let
\Lambda_{h}\colon\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\rightarrow\LL^{2}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
be the GNS map for
h
. As as
\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
is in standard position on
\LL^{2}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
, the set
\{\omega_{\Lambda_{h}(a),\Lambda_{h}(b)}\,|\,a,b\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\}
is dense in
\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
. As each member of
\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
is analytic for the modular automorphism group of
h
, this agrees in fact with the set
\{\omega_{\Lambda_{h}(a),\Lambda_{h}(\mathbbm{1})}\,|\,a\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\}
. Notice that
\omega_{\Lambda_{h}(a),\Lambda_{h}(\mathbbm{1})}
is the functional
h(a^{*}\cdot)
.

For each
\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold{\Gamma}})
there is a positive invertible operator
\uprho_{\alpha}
related to the possible non-traciality of the Haar integral
h
, see
[
51
, Section 1.7]
. We choose and fix a basis of
\mathsf{H}_{\alpha}
such that
\uprho_{\alpha}
is diagonal. We define the
Woronowicz characters

\{f_{z}\,|\,z\in\mathbb{C}\}
by the relation
(f_{z}\otimes\textnormal{id})(U^{\alpha})=\uprho_{\alpha}^{z},
valid for each
\alpha
. The modular automorphism group is then implemented as

\sigma_{z}^{h}(a)=f_{iz}\star a\star f_{iz}\quad(a\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}),z\in\mathbb{C}),

or equivalently,
(\sigma^{h}_{z}\otimes\textnormal{id})(U^{\alpha})=(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\uprho_{\alpha}^{iz})U^{\alpha}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\uprho_{\alpha}^{iz})
. Similarly, the scaling group is implemented as

\tau_{z}(a)=f_{-iz}\star a\star f_{iz}\quad(a\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}),z\in\mathbb{C}),

or equivalently,
(\tau_{z}\otimes\textnormal{id})(U^{\alpha})=(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\uprho_{\alpha}^{iz})U^{\alpha}(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\uprho_{\alpha}^{-iz})
. As we assume that
\uprho_{\alpha}
is diagonal, say with entries
\uprho_{\alpha,i}\,(1\leq i\leq\dim(\alpha))
, we get

(6.1)

\tau_{z}(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=(\uprho_{\alpha,i})^{iz}(\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{-iz}U^{\alpha}_{i,j}.

The algebra
\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
is isomorphic to the direct sum of full matrix algebras
\M_{\dim(\alpha)}
indexed by
\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
, and
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
is isomorphic to the direct product of these matrix algebras. Given
a\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
we write
a=(a^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}
where
a^{\alpha}\in\M_{\dim(\alpha)}
, and similarly for
\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
. With respect to this isomorphism,

(6.2)

\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}U^{\alpha}_{i,j}\otimes e^{\alpha}_{i,j}\in\M\big(\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\otimes\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\big),

where
\{e^{\alpha}_{i,j}\}_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}
are the matrix units of the matrix algebra
\M_{\dim(\alpha)}\subseteq\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
.

We start with a result expressing the action of
\Theta^{l}(a)\in\CB^{\sigma}(\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}))
, for
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
, on matrix elements.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any
a=(a^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))\subseteq\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
with
a^{\alpha}=[a^{\alpha}_{i,j}]_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}
we have

\Theta^{l}(a)(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=\sum_{k=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}a^{\alpha}_{i,k}U^{\alpha}_{k,j}\qquad(\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}),1\leq i,j\leq\dim(\alpha)).

Proof.

Let
x\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
and set
\omega=h(x\cdot)\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
.
Recall that
a\widehat{\lambda}(\omega)=\widehat{\lambda}(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\omega))
, equivalently,
a(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}})=(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\omega)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}})
.
Using the expression for
\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}}
from (
6.2
), it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}\langle U^{\alpha}_{k,j},\omega\rangle a^{\alpha}_{i,k}e^{\alpha}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}\sum_{k,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}\langle U^{\alpha}_{k,j},\omega\rangle ae^{\alpha}_{k,j}

\displaystyle=a(\omega\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=(\Theta^{l}(a)_{*}(\omega)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}\langle\Theta^{l}(a)(U^{\alpha}_{i,j}),\omega\rangle e^{\alpha}_{i,j}.

By density, this holds for all
\omega
, and so we conclude
\Theta^{l}(a)(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=\sum_{k=1}^{\dim(\alpha)}a^{\alpha}_{i,k}U^{\alpha}_{k,j}
, as claimed.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

Later, see Proposition
6.5
, we shall consider
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
with
\Theta^{l}(a)
unit preserving. Let
e
denote the trivial representation of
\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}
, so
\dim(e)=1
and
U^{e}=\mathbbm{1}\otimes 1
. From Lemma
6.1
, for such an
a
, we see that
\mathbbm{1}=\Theta^{l}(a)(\mathbbm{1})=a^{e}_{1,1}\mathbbm{1}
and so
a^{e}_{1,1}=1
. Further, as the Haar integral
h
annihilates all coefficients of all irreducible representations except
e
, and as
\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
is dense in
\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
, it follows that
h\circ\Theta^{l}(a)=h
.

For discrete quantum groups we will also look at a central variation of AP. We denote by
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\subseteq\mathrm{c}_{0}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
the dense subspace of elements
x=(x^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}
such that
x^{\alpha}=0
for all but finitely many
\alpha
. From the description of
\mathrm{W}^{\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}}
in (
6.2
) it is clear that we have
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\subseteq\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
. Notice that the centre of
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
, denoted
\mathcal{Z}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
, consists of families of matrices
x=(x^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Irr(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})}
such that each
x^{\alpha}\in\M_{\dim(\alpha)}
is a scalar multiple of the identity.

Definition 6.3
.

We say that a discrete quantum group
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
has the
central approximation property (central AP)
if there is a net
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\cap\mathcal{Z}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
-topology of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
.

It is clear from the definitions that central AP implies AP. At first sight, it might seem more natural to use
\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
instead of
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
in
Definition
6.3
, and indeed, this alternative definition (for other approximation properties) is taken in
[
9
, Definition 7.1]
. However, in terms
of applications, and also from the point of view of representation categories, working with
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})

is in fact the most appropriate choice. We do not know if these two properties are equivalent (a naive approximation typically produces an element which is not central). Let us point out that the examples considered in
[
9
]
actually do end up working with
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
.
We will discuss the relation of central AP to properties of the representation category
\Rep(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
in Section
8
.

Remark 6.4
.

We shall say that
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
is
finitely supported
if
a\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
. Of course, we have
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\subseteq\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\subseteq\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
. For
a\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
, it follows from Lemma
6.1
that
\Theta^{l}(a)(U^{\alpha}_{i,j})=0
for all but finitely many
\alpha
. Hence
\Theta^{l}(a)
restricted to
\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
is a finite-rank map, and so by continuity,
\Theta^{l}(a)
restricted to
\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
is finite-rank, and hence by normality,
\Theta^{l}(a)
is also finite-rank.

In the next result we show that whenever a discrete quantum group has AP, then this is implemented by a net of elements with convenient properties.

Proposition 6.5
.

Assume that
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
is a discrete quantum group with AP. Then there is a net
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements in
\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
such that

•

a_{i}\xrightarrow[i\in I]{}\mathbbm{1}
in
(\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})),w^{*})
,

•

every
a_{i}
is invariant under the scaling group of
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
and modular automorphism groups of the left/right Haar integrals,

•

every
\Theta^{l}(a_{i})
is star and unit preserving.

If
\mathbbold{\Gamma}
has central AP then we can additionally assume that
a_{i}\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})\cap\mathcal{Z}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
.

For the proof of Proposition
6.5
we shall need two lemmas. For any operator space
X
, we denote by
\kappa\colon X^{*}\widehat{\otimes}X\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
the canonical completely contractive map
\omega\otimes x\mapsto\langle\omega,x\rangle
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathsf{H}
be a Hilbert space, let
\M,\N
be von Neumann algebras, and let

v\in\bigl(\M\bar{\otimes}\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\bar{\otimes}\N\bar{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})\bigr)\widehat{\otimes}\bigl(\M_{*}\widehat{\otimes}\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\widehat{\otimes}\N_{*}\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}\bigr).

The bounded linear functional

\Omega_{v}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))\ni a\mapsto\kappa\big(((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id}^{\otimes 2})\otimes\textnormal{id}^{\otimes 4})v\big)\in\mathbb{C}

belongs to
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
, and we have
\|\Omega_{v}\|\leq\|v\|
.

Proof.

Since
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
is closed in
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))^{*}
, it is enough to consider
v=x\otimes(\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{3}\otimes\omega_{4})
for
x\in\M\bar{\otimes}\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\bar{\otimes}\N\bar{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H}),\omega_{1}\in\M_{*},\omega_{2}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}),\omega_{3}\in\N_{*},\omega_{4}\in\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\ell^{1}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
be the counit of
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
. Define
y=(\omega_{1}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\omega_{3}\otimes\textnormal{id})x\in\LL^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\bar{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})
. For
a\in\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
we have

\begin{split}&\quad\;\langle\Omega_{v},a\rangle=\kappa\bigl((\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id}^{\otimes 2})x\otimes(\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{3}\otimes\omega_{4})\bigr)\\
&=\langle(\textnormal{id}\otimes\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id}^{\otimes 2})x,\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{3}\otimes\omega_{4}\rangle=\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})y,\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{4}\rangle\\
&=\langle(\Theta^{l}(a\star\varepsilon)\otimes\textnormal{id})y,\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{4}\rangle=\langle a,\Omega_{y,\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{4},\varepsilon}\rangle\end{split}

by the definition of
\Omega_{y,\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{4},\varepsilon}
(see (
4.3
)) hence
\Omega_{v}=\Omega_{y,\omega_{2}\otimes\omega_{4},\varepsilon}
and the claim follows from Proposition
4.7
.
∎

In the following, recall that a
mean
on
\mathbb{R}
is a state
m_{\mathbb{R}}
on
\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
which is invariant under the translation action of
\mathbb{R}
. Such a state exists as the group
\mathbb{R}
is abelian and hence amenable.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
m_{\mathbb{R}}
be a mean on
\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathsf{H}
be a Hilbert space, and let
x\in\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H}),\rho\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
.
Then

\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}\colon\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))\ni a\mapsto m_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(t\mapsto\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(x),\rho\circ(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\rangle\bigr)\in\mathbb{C}

defines a bounded functional on
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
.
Furthermore,
\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
and
\|\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}\|\leq\|x\|\|\rho\|
.

Proof.

As
t\mapsto\rho\circ(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})
is norm continuous, it follows that the function
t\mapsto\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(x),\rho\circ(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\rangle
is continuous, and bounded, and so we can indeed apply
m_{\mathbb{R}}
to it. The only nontrivial claim is that
\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}
is a normal functional.

In order to verify this take
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Using the canonical complete contraction
\kappa
we can write

\begin{split}\langle(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id})(\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(x),\rho\circ(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\rangle=\kappa(\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigl((\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(x)\otimes\rho\circ(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigr).\end{split}

Let
x=U^{\alpha}_{i,j}\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}),y=U^{\beta}_{k,l}\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
and set
\rho=h(y\cdot)\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
. As
h
is
(\hat{\tau}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
invariant, it follows that
(\rho\circ\hat{\tau}_{t})(z)=h(y\hat{\tau}_{t}(z))=h(\hat{\tau}_{t}(\hat{\tau}_{-t}(y)z))=h(\hat{\tau}_{-t}(y)z)
for each
z\in\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
, and so
\rho\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}=h(\hat{\tau}_{-t}(y)\cdot)
. From (
6.1
) we hence see that

\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x)\otimes\rho\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}=(\uprho_{\alpha,i})^{-it}(\uprho_{\alpha,j})^{it}(\uprho_{\beta,k})^{-it}(\uprho_{\beta,l})^{it}x\otimes\rho.

In particular, function
\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x)\otimes\rho\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}
takes values in a finite dimensional subspace of
\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\odot\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
. For such a function we can define its mean
m_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(t\mapsto\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x)\otimes\rho\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}\bigr)\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\odot\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
by choosing a basis in the image, and taking mean of
t
-dependent coefficients (it will not depend on the choice of basis). By linearity, we obtain
m_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(t\mapsto\hat{\tau}_{-t}(x)\otimes\rho\circ\hat{\tau}_{t}\bigr)\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\odot\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
for any
x,y\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
. By linearity again, given
x\in\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\odot\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})
and
\rho\in h(\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\,\cdot\,)\odot\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
, it follows that

v=m_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(t\mapsto(\hat{\tau}_{-t}\otimes\textnormal{id})(x)\otimes\rho\circ(\hat{\tau}_{t}\otimes\textnormal{id})\bigr)\in\bigl(\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\odot\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H})\bigr)\odot\bigl(\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\odot\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}\bigr)

and we have

\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}(a)=\kappa\bigl((\Theta^{l}(a)\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id}\otimes\textnormal{id})v\bigr)=\Omega_{v}(a).

Consequently we have
\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}\in Q^{l}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
by Lemma
6.6
. Furthermore,

\|\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}\|\leq\|v\|\leq\|x\|\|\rho\|.

General elements
x\in\mathrm{C}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{H}),\rho\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})\widehat{\otimes}\B(\mathsf{H})_{*}
can be approximated in norm by
x,\rho
as above, hence
\Omega^{\tau}_{x,\rho}
is a normal functional, using again that
Q^{l}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))\subseteq\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))^{*}
is a closed subspace.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

By assumption, there is a net
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
which converges to
\mathbbm{1}
in the weak
∗
-topology of
\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma}))
. For each
i\in I
there is
\omega_{i}\in\LL^{1}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
with
a_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}(\omega_{i})
, and there are
\xi_{i},\eta_{i}\in\LL^{2}(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma})
with
\omega_{i}=\omega_{\xi_{i},\eta_{i}}
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
we may choose
\xi_{i,n},\eta_{i,n}\in\Lambda_{h}(\Pol(\widehat{\mathbbold}{\Gamma}))
with
\|\xi_{i}-\xi_{i,n}\|\leq\epsilon_{1}
and
\|\eta_{i}-\eta_{i,n}\|\leq\epsilon_{2}
, where

\epsilon_{1}=\tfrac{1}{1+2n\|\eta_{i}\|},\quad\epsilon_{2}=\tfrac{1}{2n(\epsilon_{1}+\|\xi_{i}\|)}.

Set
a_{i,n}=\widehat{\lambda}(\omega_{\xi_{i,n},\eta_{i,n}})
, so that
a_{i,n}\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
, and

\displaystyle\|a_{i}-a_{i,n}\|_{\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})}

\displaystyle=\|\omega_{i}-\omega_{\xi_{i,n},\eta_{i,n}}\|\leq\|\omega_{\xi_{i},\eta_{i}}-\omega_{\xi_{i,n},\eta_{i}}\|+\|\omega_{\xi_{i,n},\eta_{i}}-\omega_{\xi_{i,n},\eta_{i,n}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{1}\|\eta_{i}\|+\epsilon_{2}\|\xi_{i,n}\|\leq\epsilon_{1}\|\eta_{i}\|+\epsilon_{2}\big(\epsilon_{1}+\|\xi_{i}\|\big)\leq\tfrac{1}{n}.

Equipping
I\times\mathbb{N}
with the product order, it follows that
a_{i,n}\xrightarrow[(i,n)\in I\times\mathbb{N}]{}\mathbbm{1}
in
(\M^{l}_{cb}(\A(\mathbbold{\Gamma})),w^{*})
.

Fix
(i,n)\in I\times\mathbb{N}
and choose
m_{\mathbb{R}}\in\LL^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})^{*}
, a mean on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
b_{i,n}
be the unique element of
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
with

\langle b_{i,n},\omega\rangle=m_{\mathbb{R}}(t\mapsto\langle\tau_{t}(a_{i,n}),\omega\rangle)\qquad(\omega\in\ell^{1}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})).

As each
\tau_{t}
leaves each matrix block
\M_{\dim(\alpha)}\subseteq\ell^{\infty}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
invariant, it follows that
b_{i,n}\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
, because
a_{i,n}\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma})
. Next, we set

c_{i,n}=\tfrac{1}{2}(b_{i,n}+R(b_{i,n})^{*})\in\mathrm{c}_{00}(\mathbbold{\Gamma}).

Clearly each
b_{i,n}
, and consequently each
c_{i,n}
, is invariant under
(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
. Thus
c_{i,n}
is analytic for
(\tau_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
, and so
c_{i,n}\in D(S^{-1})
and
S^{-1}(c_{i,n})=R(c_{i,n})
. Then as
\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}=\nabla_{\psi}^{-it}=P^{it}
, see
[
41
, Lemma 6.2]
, each
c_{i,n}
is also invariant under the modular automorphism group.
Since
